mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, you and, above all, president juncker, have repeatedly promised to put forward proposals to ensure equal pay for equal work and to be the champion of social dialogue, that is to say, that employers and trade unions should be the driving forces behind wage movements, not politics. a week ago a commission proposal was leaked which went in the opposite direction and caused a lot of emotion. we have now received a new proposal, which i am grateful for, which looks better, which is worth evaluating, and we shall see what the analysis gives. but what we do know is that the eu will need more labour market mobility, and that makes equal pay for equal work, not pay by where you come from, even more important. mrs thyssen has repeatedly pointed out the unreasonableness of the fact that we used to have some kind of biological pay, i.e. pay by sex. women were automatically paid less than men. what you're sticking to now is geographically-based pay, which means where you come from is more important than what job you do. it's ridiculous. it's a thousand and nine hundred by my standards. this must change, and this must be the result of the new scrutiny you are making and the new proposals you are presenting. we in our employment committee are prepared to work with you, but we cannot go our separate ways.
madam president, i would like to thank you. thank you, madam commissioner, and thank you to the commission for this initiative. of course, it will take a lot for europe to become a social triple-a part of the world. what are the conditions? we live in a time of refugee crisis, right-wing extremism on the rise, questioning of freedom of speech and media freedom - all this on top of an economic crisis that is far from over. and at the same time, social partners are not getting involved in the way they should be in order for us to solve the problems. i just can't do it without them. politics cannot do it alone. i think the experience of the last few years is a clear example of this. eurofound has produced a very interesting report, which i would like you to read. it is about what has happened in the crisis countries that were treated very harshly by the troika and were forced out of their collective bargaining systems. these countries are still suffering more than others, and that is because the nerve endings of the social partners have been destroyed by economism. so, more of a social force, let the people go free so that they can be involved and work together with politicians and others and unions to actually restore the spirit of life. that's what this is about.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we are opposed to turkish membership of the eu for several reasons. the first and most important is that the country is not in europe. only three per cent of turkey is entering europe, and i hope you can see for yourselves what this could mean for the eu to have a border with syria, iraq and iran. secondly, there is now blackmail against the eu, demanding more money and visa facilitation for turkish citizens and a quick-file into membership to help at all. repression of minorities is common in turkey, for example against the kurds. there's no respect for human rights. the attitude towards freedom of the press is, to say the least, alarming when it comes to media and freedom of expression, and one can note with great concern an islamist development in the country in recent years with the ruling akp party. turkey's gdp is a fraction of my own country's gdp, and we have enough of spending on the eu as it is. i do not want to add any more burden to the swedish taxpayer's burden, because that is where it ends up. the eu, in its panic about not being able to deal with the migration crisis, would give in to blackmail is the wrong way to approach this problem. we are open to dialogue and cooperation with turkey to solve the problems, but let me be clear when i say that it also ends there.
mr president, i would like to thank you. most of this has already been said, and i can see that the criticism is widespread in this house, both from my green colleagues and from the vast majority of others. i hope that the commission and the council will take up this criticism. i wonder what you're really thinking. you sell out our core european values and you propose that we violate the geneva convention in exchange for a barter of refugees. we're going to trade syrians for syrians. this is absurd. i want to know which syrians are being sent back, and why, and who's being brought here. i want to know how the asylum cases are to be tried, without violating the right of asylum, and who decides a case. i want to know how the right of asylum will be maintained in relation to all other nationalities, and i have many questions to which i believe you do not have answers. this is a bad proposal, which i hope will fall on its own folly. we must take a joint responsibility in europe. we will not barter with neighboring countries. turkey is also not a safe country to send refugees back to.
mr president, i would like to thank you. 'today was a good day', said angela merkel yesterday about the negotiations with the erdoğan regime. it's true in a european laughter-mirror room. yesterday was a good day for the dealers at the turkish bazaar. yesterday was a good day for human traffickers. one by one, it's called. if 10 syrian boat refugees are intercepted in the mediterranean and returned to turkey, 10 syrians will be sent from turkey to europe. but not the same people. it's a bargain and a shaking worthy of a slave market. it's trading people. it's a human rights bargain. it does not seem that merkel and rutte's colleagues in the council are buying their proposal. orbán has already threatened to veto. the problem is that merkel's opponents have even worse solutions. or none at all. no, yesterday was not a good day. it's been a long time since human rights had a good day. in my europe, you act with wisdom and responsibility and not as a hostage to erdogan.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of the directive on procedural safeguards for children suspected or accused in criminal proceedings because 1 million children are involved in criminal justice every year in europe and they are particularly vulnerable. they need special protection and assistance to understand what is at stake, the law and their rights. while criminal law is a national competence, the minimum standards laid out in this directive will improve legal certainty. i believe that mandatory assistance by a lawyer, children’s right to an individual assessment of their familiar and social situation, specialised training for justices, prosecutors, and other professionals involved in criminal justice are crucial safeguards and represent a step forward in the direction of guaranteeing a fair trial for children in the eu. indeed, as co-chair of the intergroup on children’s rights in the european parliament, i am happy that the best interests of the child is at the core of the directive, which provides a comprehensive protection system at all stages for children involved in criminal proceedings, putting children’s rights at the top of the eu political agenda.
‒ today the european parliament voted on its first proposal to the council for the eu budget for 2017. europe faces many challenges and the eu budget is crucial to meeting them. unfortunately, the committee on budgets, which drew up the report, wants to increase spending in virtually all areas instead of re-prioritizing the budget and focusing on core issues. the report also proposes to relax the budgetary rules. the report also calls for an eu tax, something the christian democrats clearly rejected in the election campaign. the christian democrats are clear that the long-term ambition is to gradually reduce eu spending on agricultural, labour market, regional and industrial policies. i therefore voted against the report. however, in separate votes, i highlighted support for more money to address the migration challenges.
in writing. - i voted against the guidelines. i believe that the eu budget is too large and should be cut. the report supports the agreement with turkey on the issue of the expulsion of refugees who have arrived in the eu. an agreement that is, among other things, contrary to the binding geneva convention on the status of refugees. support is also provided for the disbursement of at least eur 3 billion to turkey, a country which daily violates human rights and democratic principles. the report supports the extremely costly iter fusion energy project when the eu should instead invest heavily in renewable energy and energy efficiency and savings. the report states that there is still strong support for the regional and structural funds, which have also long been subject to widespread fraud. these are some of the reasons why i choose to vote no.
‒ the report sets out general guidelines for the preparation of the 2017 budget; i agree with the report's guidelines in principle. there are a number of problematic lines in the report; i think, for example, that school milk and school fruit should be dealt with at national level; i think that the globalisation funds should be abolished; some lines on the eu's own resources were too far-reaching. in future votes on specific budget lines i shall vote against these items. since there was no opportunity to vote on the individual texts in this report and since the report contains important political signals, such as the need for the budget to take account of the global climate agreement concluded in paris last year and the importance of full funding of the eu's research programmes, i nevertheless chose to vote in favour of the report as a whole.
‒ we cannot support the european parliament's demand for higher expenditure in 2017. instead, we want to prioritise eu spending to focus on those things that strengthen europe's competitiveness and create the conditions for growth and jobs. the eu should give priority to the infrastructure of the single market, research and our long-term capacity to develop an effective foreign policy and jointly strengthen control of our borders. we therefore welcome the priorities in the area of migration and research and development. but we want to reduce agricultural aid, industrial subsidies and those parts of regional policy which make well-functioning economies dependent on subsidies. we cannot support the demands to make the eu's budgetary rules more flexible, to increase the flexibility of the stability pact, to demand a larger long-term budget or to give the eu taxation rights. it is not a bigger eu budget that strengthens europe's competitiveness and the conditions for growth and jobs. it requires structural reforms, balanced public finances, deregulation and a reduction in bureaucracy.
‒ i have voted against the report. improvements have been made to the original text, but the basic tone of the report remains essentially about the interests of businesses in simplification and reduction of administrative burden. i am in favour of reforms that reduce the administrative burden on businesses, but not if simplification of legislation has negative consequences in areas such as the working environment and the protection of workers, or limits the legislator's ability to regulate. i also believe that the european commission is given too much power and control in the proposed decisionmaking process. i also believe that the legislative process would be much more transparent. transparency is essential for democracy to work. for these reasons, i am voting against.
the resolution stresses the problems with the current system and the commission's delay in providing parliament with an evaluation of the agreements currently in force. it is important that we move as quickly as possible from a situation where we base the fight against smuggling, counterfeiting and tax evasion on individual agreements with private companies to an international comprehensive set of rules which are the same for everyone. i therefore want the commission to ensure that we leave these agreements as soon as possible and that the agreements currently in force are not renegotiated or extended.
in writing. we do not believe that the eu should renew the current cooperation agreement with pmi. the cooperation agreement is not effective in tackling the real problem of the illegal trade in tobacco products in the eu, which consists almost exclusively of the smuggling of unlabeled cigarettes. instead, we are concerned that an extension of the pmi cooperation agreement is incompatible with the who framework convention on tobacco control, which aims to protect public health policy from the influence of the tobacco industry, and would instead legitimise the tobacco industry and give it undesirable influence. in order to ensure the strongest possible protection of public health, in today's vote we voted against individual resolutions in favour of the joint resolution (rc-b8-0312/2016) tabled.
prime minister of sweden.– mr president, i thank you very much for the invitation to be here today. it is an honour for me and it is a pleasure. ‘it was the best of times, it was the worst of times, it was the age of wisdom, it was the age of foolishness, it was the epoch of belief, it was the epoch of incredulity, it was the season of light, it was the season of darkness, it was the spring of hope, it was the winter of despair.’. these are the opening lines of dickens’ ‘a tale of two cities’. he is describing europe at the end of the 18th century – but he could just as well have been talking about our own times. we have indeed weathered a winter of despair: of terrorists and extremists attempting to attack our fundaments; of russia continuing to try to move european borders using armed force; of our union not being able to cope with youth unemployment, social hopelessness and the global refugee crisis. but there are also some rays of hope in the world. the global climate agreement, the new sustainable development goals, and a large part of the african countries rising from poverty: these all show that we live in a time when global cooperation really can make a difference. and the eu also has a chance at a spring of hope if standing strong and united against external threats, if tackling common challenges with the collective strength required to resolve them, if clearing away the last ruins of the financial crisis and starting to build strong societies again. i was born in 1957, the same year as the treaty of rome. my generation was given vast opportunities and had great hope for the future. to many of us, the world seemed to be ours to explore and to make into a better place. now i cannot see a greater duty than to give the same opportunity and hope to the young generation of today. and i know that the european union can be a driving force for that, if we stand up for three central values of our union: that is democracy, cooperation and also added value for our citizens. let me start with democracy. we are currently strengthening our joint action against terrorism following cowardly and appalling attacks against our society. many of these actions that we are taking are tough, but they are necessary. but the work to defend democracy is a much greater task. defending democracy is standing up for the safety of our jewish citizens at a time when anti-semitic threats, hatred and violence once again echo throughout europe. it is never accepting or spreading populists’ and extremists’ demonisation of muslims, even if it scores cheap points with the electorate. it is working for the rights of the roma and of all minorities who are held back by prejudice and poverty. defending democracy is also defending free and independent media, freedom of reporting and freedom of the press, because we know that politics always benefits from hard scrutiny. defending democracy is respecting every person’s right to meet, to be tried in free and fair trials, to move freely in society and to love whoever they want. if we do not defend these democratic values, we will lose europe as we know it. that brings me to my second point, which is cooperation. rarely have so many people been displaced in the world as they are right now, but rarely have so few european countries stepped up and been prepared to act. during a two-month period last autumn, sweden took in some 80 000 people at a rate that is equivalent to 25 million asylum seekers annually in the european union as a whole. this brought our reception system to the brink of collapse and, in the absence of working european solutions, it forced us to take unilateral action. now the schengen agreement and the free movement it creates is a foundation pillar of the european union, and crucial for our economies. but if we do not act, and act fast, we might lose it. i see a lot of words and plans to move forward, but far too little action. i therefore urge all the member states to take their responsibility. it is time to act, to ensure control of our external borders, to enhance our cooperation with turkey on the basis of international law, to implement the redistribution of refugees within the european union, fight human smuggling and also strengthen our common foreign policy to put a stop to the causes of the refugee crisis. so we must move from chaos to control, otherwise we risk european cooperation as we know it. and, looking ahead, it is time to admit that the dublin regulation is not working and needs to be replaced. if we do not create a new, common and sustainable asylum system, more countries will be forced to act unilaterally, which will hurt mobility, trade and – most of all – it will hurt the human beings who are fleeing. sweden is ready – and will act – for a new asylum system in the eu that is based on equal distribution, and in which asylum is sought in the eu – not in an individual country. quite simply, to put it shortly, if we are to share an external border and have free movement between our countries, we must also share a system of asylum reception. to be honest, i cannot see how countries that do not participate in the common asylum system can participate in schengen cooperation either. this brings me to my third point, and that is the added value for our citizens. now we have shown that we are ready to work hard to keep together, as we have recently done to help to keep the united kingdom within our union. but the complaints against the european union all over our continent should not be brushed aside. these complaints show us the dire need to emphasise that special something that keeps us together – the european spirit, the shared value of cooperation for our citizens. we need to demonstrate the value of 28 countries and 508 million inhabitants joining together: to address challenges such as climate change that no country can manage alone, in this time of global interdependence; to create an even larger common market, in this time of global competition; and to generate greater security for our people, in this time of global unease. but if eu cooperation is to truly gain the broad popular support that is needed to stay strong in the 21st century, we must have a union that works much harder for the wellbeing of the people, the citizens. in a world where 62 people own as much as 3.5 billion people, in such a world, we need a european union that works for equality and everyone’s chance to pursue their innermost hopes for a good life. this is why we need to build a social europe that strengthens people through more secure jobs, better living conditions and strengthens enterprises through more secure access to citizens with purchasing power. because when people and enterprises are strengthened, economies grow. in a social europe, the eu is a prime advocate for good terms and conditions for everyone working in the union, making sure that workers are never forced to compete against each other through lower wages or poorer working conditions. ensuring that in a tricky world of global subcontractors, there is always someone who is responsible for ensuring that you are safe at work. in a social europe, the eu should work to unleash the energy of a growing labour force. if women were employed to the same extent as men in the european union, gdp could grow or increase some 12% by 2030. so the eu must not only act as a sentinel for sound finances, it must also act as a driving force for women’s entry into the labour market and a truly equal world of work. this is as ethically right as it is economically smart. in a social europe, the eu should take a collective stand against the youth unemployment that is breaking down the youth and also breaking down our economies and our societies’ faith in the future. so we should prioritise investment in education, expand exchanges of european students between not only higher education institutions but also upper secondary schools. and create the jobs for the future for young people via social investment, ambitious free trade agreements and a digital internal market. this is the kind of social europe that can win the hearts of the people in europe. the commission’s proposal for amending the posting of workers directive and the development of the social pillar show that the work is finally and truly under way, which i welcome. taking steps towards more decent working conditions and social progress is paramount. so let us continue onwards and upwards. let us come together for a summit and draw up a strategy to move this idea of a social europe forward, and i would be pleased and honoured to host such a summit in sweden next year in 2017. (applause)we are working for an eu that works hard for the size of the market, yes, but even harder for the wellbeing of its people. i see parliament and the commission as key actors on all three issues. i can feel in this very hall and in our union that we carry the ability to create the same hope that i felt as the generation of 1957, for the generations of 1997, 2007, 2017, and many generations to come. so europe’s winter of despair has been long and hard. but if we stand up for democracy when democracy is tested, if we cooperate and turn words into deeds to address the migration crisis, and if we create a social europe that increases the wellbeing in people’s everyday lives, then we will be able – in these best of times and in these worst of times – to create a new spring of hope in europe. (applause)
vice-president of the commission.– mr president, president juncker has asked me to replace him in this debate and convey his greetings. yes, it is the best of times and it is the worst of times but, above all, it is the time for us in europe to stand up for our values and be counted. prime minister, i am very honoured to welcome you in this chamber, where you have just done exactly that. thank you. sweden is a country that has shaped its own destiny with values and the conviction that we can live and work together and provide for a fair society. you have also influenced those beyond your borders – the rest of us in europe. i will just recall that, together with your scandinavian partners, you were the first to understand the value of free movement: it was the then nordic council which, in the 1950s, launched free travel and the original labour market for the first time. your achievement has served you very well and it is serving europe very well. today, on behalf of our president, i would like to pay tribute to sweden’s response to the refugee crisis. your country has long received the highest number of people seeking protection, way before the current crisis started. your solidarity is a shining light. when a country so far north in the union is affected so directly by events to the south, we know we are in this together. no country can manage this crisis alone and no country should be left alone, be it greece, sweden, austria or germany. no country can carry the weight on its own. together we have to protect our borders. together we have to help people who depend on us for their survival. we recognise the effort sweden has made and we have suspended the relocation of refugees to sweden from greece and italy. that is pragmatic and fair. today, as you have said, in most parts of europe we need to restore order. we need to restore the schengen system. your country knows the cost of life without schengen. i was struck by how costly it is to have border controls on the øresund bridge between sweden and denmark – the costs run into hundreds of millions of euros a month. last friday, the commission published a roadmap setting out the steps to restore schengen to full health and lift all internal borders by december 2016 at the latest. this gives us an opportunity to make schengen more resilient. we have to learn from the crisis that the system works only if we all participate: otherwise it does not work at all. that is why, obviously, we all have to pull together. at the commission we are not interested in pointing the finger. we are interested in solutions and in helping member states – all member states – to bear a shared responsibility for restoring and protecting schengen. prime minister, let me turn to a second topic which you touched upon and which also has to do with values in europe: europe’s social model. it is one of our greatest achievements. far more than a way to organise our economy, it is part of who we are, it is part of our treaties and it is part of our identity. sweden has written entire chapters in this story. listening to you and your government, you are holding the pen and still writing. thank you for that. what we have demonstrated to the rest of the world is that our social market economy can have prosperity and social justice going hand in hand. my former employer, the world bank, called the european union a lifestyle superpower because of the standards we have established for our citizens – standards in gender equality, in public health and in the environment, and above all in our welfare system. our challenge is to adapt this model to a world which is changing much faster than in the years that you and i were born. with this in mind – the need to constantly adapt – we at the commission have launched a proposal for a european pillar of social rights. it sets out principles and practices to ensure that work is decent and fair and that we meet our goals of equal opportunity, fair conditions and proper social protection. it is about delivering fairness for individuals and also building resiliency into our labour market and our economy. by revealing the gaps in the current legislation, the pillar will show what measures we still need to take. let me make one final point. over many decades, sweden has embodied european values, values of equality, values of compassion for people seeking protection, and today you are showing that these values are very strong. we are going to respond to the greatest test of our time. these values are the light that we ought to follow. there is a european solution. and that, moreover, is the only solution. i would like to thank you, prime minister, and to thank sweden, for being such a key part of it.
for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. madam vice-president and mr prime minister, welcome to the house. it is gratifying to hear a social democrat prime minister, as well as other representatives of sweden, so clearly emphasize that sweden stands behind european cooperation, strong and stable. we live in a time when the crises coming from outside - russia, syria, russia in syria, isis, terrorism, climate change, refugees - are challenging the very values that are the foundation of our society. only a strong europe can deal with this and that is why i would like to say: don't ever say, we have to solve all these problems next week or else the cooperation has failed we will need this cooperation for a very long time, because the problems we face are bigger than there being a simple solution and a decision. instead of always asking what europe can do for us, we must ask what we can do for europe. i know that wording has been used before, but that's what we have to do. on social issues, do not weaken european cooperation by taking them from national parliaments to the european parliament. then we have the conflicts, then we have the over-regulation, then we have the bureaucracy that people all over europe are turning against. the first thing that comes out of the british debate, and which many of us in sweden also share, is that there is too much bureaucracy. we will reduce bureaucracy and we will make it easier by removing the jungle of national regulations that impede economic development. a free trade agreement with the united states, stable public finances. i would like the prime minister to speak to those in his own party groups who believe that you get more money by spending more money than you have. the refugee question: our country has taken on a very great responsibility. we have to make sure that there is a burden sharing. we have to make sure we follow the rules. the same is true of the öresund bridge. when we introduce controls, we should follow the rules and not introduce carrier liability. as far as refugees are concerned, we must ensure that other countries take responsibility, that borders are respected and that the necessary responsibility is taken throughout europe. in the case of turkey, clear demands for democracy and freedom of the press. in this cooperation we must make turkey more democratic and more rule of law if we are to protect the people whose fate is at stake. and remember, mr. prime minister, we are here to make europe strong. it's gonna take a lot of work over a long period of time, but together, we're gonna get through this.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to welcome the prime minister to the european parliament today. it's nice of you to share this afternoon with us. as we know, sweden is a medium-sized eu member state, and i would urge the prime minister to take advantage of this. i know that you in the swedish government agree with me that the dublin regulation, the cornerstone of a functioning european asylum policy, must be reformed. i will be leading that work here in parliament and look forward to our continued cooperation on this. but now i ask you, stefan löfven, to take the helm of the council of ministers and convince the other governments of the need to create a solidarity-based, sustainable asylum policy in the eu. only then can we, together, bring about the real change that is needed now more than ever. unfortunately, sweden has gone from being a country that has dared to take up arms in the council of ministers, and which with its weight and credibility in asylum and migration matters has been able to influence other member states in the right direction, to being increasingly seen as a country that follows the tide rather passively and repeats the mantra: 'people on the run are someone else's responsibility and not ours'. that is not the way to lead the way towards a common european responsibility. the agreement with turkey on monday will not resolve the situation in the long term. there are strong indications that its substantial parts are contrary to the geneva convention and also to the eu treaties. even if many of the people who are now trying to get to the eu are stopped, they will find other ways to get to the eu, and not everyone can stay in greece. without a european asylum policy in which everyone participates, no member state will be able to do this. we will see repeated examples of the greek tragedy unfolding across the eu. instead of outsourcing our responsibility for refugees to turkey, the eu should now secure its own external borders, create legal safe routes for refugees and ensure an asylum system where responsibility is shared fairly between all 28 countries. migration cooperation with turkey can only work if there is a common border guard and a common asylum policy. stefan löfven, take the initiative now and build good coalitions with the other countries. show some leadership and tell me which countries in the council are putting the wheels on the wheel. the time for the back door in the council of ministers must be over now, because we have a good swedish tradition of openness and transparency which should also be a guide for the council. one of the eu leaders must break the race to the bottom, and i wish it were my own country's government that did it. europe simply deserves much, much more than we are doing at the moment.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. mr prime minister, welcome to the european parliament! sweden has taken a big responsibility in 2015 to take in people who have fled. that's all well and good, but then something happened in the fall and you made a deal with the right-wing opposition parties to close our borders. i think that was the wrong decision, but that's not what we're here to debate. the important thing today is that we are facing the same situation and the same challenge at european level. should europe take in refugees and organise a dignified reception, or should we claim that the eu too needs a breathing space and close our borders? what role will sweden play in that election? to me, the answers are obvious, mr. prime minister. the eu as a whole has taken in a very small proportion of the people who are fleeing today. we will of course share and receive more, with dignity, and i believe of course that sweden should be a leader in that development, in a political change of course for the eu that ensures that we do not close our borders. unfortunately, i don't see that role enough in the council. let me be clear: a new super-border guard with extraordinary powers to enforce visas and to act in third countries and to intervene without consent in a member state is not a solution. nato patrols in the mediterranean are not a solution and, last but not least, dealing with turkey with mass expulsions is not a solution. refugees are not fishing quotas that we load and unload. these are people of flesh and blood who have fled and left everything in conflict. mr. prime minister, i'm not being naive. i see that it is not possible to get orbán and some other countries on board. the eu-28 has reached its end, whatever people here say, both morally and politically. so you must have a plan b based on a coalition of willing member states, who want to go forward in close cooperation with unhcr, who were here yesterday and spoke very wisely. you can use the eu institutions even in a small coalition. finally, it is not worthy of a social democratic government here in europe or in any country to oppose refugees to welfare. it's just playing into the hands of racists, and you can't work with them. 2015 was the year that people went out and welcomed refugees. what 2016 will be, we don't know yet, but it's pretty much up to us here and up to you in the council. is it the year we built the walls or is it the year of solidarity with the refugees?
for the efdd group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. stefan löfven, you yourself have said that we in sweden have been naive in our reception of refugees. you may have inherited the government's sinking ship of migration, reinfeldt, but the irresponsible policy you have pursued for a year and a half has made the situation many times worse. because of countries like sweden, the eu's migration situation has developed into an economic, political and humanitarian chaos. today you stand here in this chamber and want to inflict on the whole of europe the consequences of your naive policy. you ask other member states to 'rescue you in case of need' when you yourself have been a bad boy. the eu member states must take a joint responsibility, you say. your government must take more responsibility and actively contribute to ending mass migration to the eu, i say. do you really believe in forced distribution? how will this solution ever work? many member states lack the resources, capacity and political will to integrate the gigantic number of migrants. country after country is turning away from the redistribution mechanism you're promoting. stefan löfven, you have to bite into that sour apple, and realize that sweden's really big problem is instead how we, on our own, should be able to expel the approximately 80,000 migrants that your government has announced will leave sweden. how are we gonna do that? we who can't even evacuate 30 asylum seekers from a bus!
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you for being here, stefan löfven. it's good and it's important. you hear the vagrant rants and all the completely unreasonable claims flying through the air. they do that a lot in this chamber, and that's why it's so nice to have you here. i would like to return to the inequality that is growing throughout europe. we have 23 million registered unemployed today, and we know that the real figure is much higher. there are those around europe - and yes, in this room too - who think that we should now face this crisis, this unemployment with low wages and worse conditions, and that we should have a labour market that is segregated between eu citizens and the newcomers who come here, who at the same time should have even worse conditions. this is despite the fact that it leads to even greater disparities and even more unequal, unfair conditions in the labour market. we social democrats want to put an end to social dumping, to wage dumping. competition with poorer conditions and low wages is dragging down everyone, not just those who are hit first. president juncker of the commission has put it this way: 'no social dumping - equal pay for equal work'. i know it's obvious, but i feel like this. can we really trust juncker to achieve this? member of the commission.– madam president, the commission welcomes the opportunity to discuss the human rights situation in the democratic republic of congo (drc) and it looks forward to the adoption of parliament’s resolution which addresses the crucial political process towards elections and the worrying situation regarding human rights and fundamental freedoms in the drc. the drc is a vast country of immense challenges, not least those of poverty and security, including gender-based violence. as sakharov prize winner dr denis mukwege has told this house, women’s bodies have become a true battlefield. it is true that the democratic republic of congo has experienced a protracted humanitarian and security crisis. the fragile peace in eastern drc depends on what has become the largest un peacekeeping operation ever. monusco is still facing a highly volatile and insecure situation. since the constitution and elections of 2006 there has been some progress. the economy expanded, some armed groups in the east were defeated or restrained, and there was an effort with much work still ahead to build viable regional institutions, but the drc is now approaching a critical milestone in its evolution. 2016 should be a presidential election year, but the opposition and civil society fear that the constitutional deadlines will not be respected. a political consensus is urgently needed on a focused set of steps to deliver inclusive and transparent elections within the framework of the constitution. this does not mean that government and opposition have to agree on every policy challenge facing the country, but it does mean they should find a common understanding on a roadmap leading to the ballot box. getting the right political climate for democracy to work is also important. unfortunately, protests and peaceful dissent have been met with repression in some cases and fundamental freedoms seem to be at stake. state security services are accused of involvement in human rights abuses and in a number of cases the judiciary has been disproportionately severe. the eu issued for this reason a statement in kinshasa on 8 march reminding the government of the need to respect its obligation. the stakes are high, the economy has been hit by falling commodity prices and an inability to expand domestic revenue, and public services suffer. this recipe for public alienation and discontent deserves our full attention. the eu has invested heavily in security, humanitarian aid and development. we are committed to a long-term partnership with the drc. it is not only material help. we have deployed intense political and diplomatic activity in support of peace, stability and reconciliation in active partnership with african regional actors and with the rest of the international community. in our comprehensive approach we have placed human rights first and we recognise the crucial role of the un as well. working closely with the united nations joint human rights office in the drc we monitor and support human rights defenders, we contribute to fighting impunity and we look to the longer term with support for security sector reform and conflict prevention, as well as human and social development. we also seek engagement with the authorities in political and policy dialogue. in conclusion, we believe that it will only be possible for the drc to move from fragility to resilience in a political culture of constitutionalism, rule of law and respect for human rights. the drc’s future belongs first and foremost to its citizens. their voice must be heard in civic discourse and at the polls and the european union will use the range of its instruments to make this happen. this resolution from parliament is therefore timely and welcome, and i am grateful for the particular attention you paid to my speech.

member of the commission.– mr president, thank you on behalf of the commission for this opportunity to speak today on the occasion of international roma day, which we celebrated last friday. discrimination against roma is still widespread across the eu. anti-gypsyism, a specific form of racism closely related to deep-rooted negative stereotyping and stigmatisation of roma, is on the rise. it is linked to the rise of radicalism and extremism in general in the eu. this trend raises serious concerns. it is crucial to take a strong stand and to do our best to stop these negative phenomena, which are incompatible with the values cherished by the european union. the commission has put in place a solid framework of policy, legal and financial instruments which, if implemented properly, should produce results on the ground. the commission has stepped up its efforts to ensure the correct implementation of legislation to combat discrimination against roma. the commission has also urged member states to demonstrate greater political will and a long-term vision to combat discrimination and promote the inclusion of roma people. the inclusion of minorities in education, the labour market and political life is one of the main ingredients of a healthy society. this year the commission launched a targeted campaign to effectively tackle stereotypes and prejudices against the roma, called ‘for roma, with roma’. it includes training for the media and close collaboration with municipalities in selected member states. the fight against discrimination against roma and anti-gypsyism – including hate speech and hate crime targeting the roma community – is included in the commission’s annual reporting on the progress made in advancing roma integration, as well as in the council recommendations on effective roma integration measures, the first ever eu legal instruments specifically referring to roma. the commission has welcomed and strongly supported the initiative of the european parliament to designate 2 august as european roma holocaust memorial day. the commission has also encouraged member states to do the same. it is our duty to acknowledge and remember all those who suffered under the nazi regime, including roma. the suffering of roma in concentration camps is a horrific chapter of human history that is less widely known. the commission will support the european parliament in its action to bring the european parliament resolution to the council for further discussion.

for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. this report on the annual reports on subsidiarity and proportionality 2012-2013 contains several good suggestions to put the eu on the right track to make the subsidiarity check work, which is very important for strengthening democracy in the eu. it also contains a number of rather bad proposals on an excess of impact assessments. i shall begin by talking about the problem with this report. impact assessments are not as easy to do as one might think. it is easy to come up with figures - not necessarily correct figures - but figures for how much it will cost a company to implement, for example, a new good environmental legislation. however, it is more difficult to quantify the socioeconomic benefits of a proposal. impact assessments risk being skewed towards inaction. the commission's proposal to have impact assessments before, during and after the work on a proposal, as proposed in this report, is far too much impact assessment and would create problems for the eu's ability to act. the good things in the report are about making the eu's subsidiarity control work, getting the yellow card which is not being used at all today to the extent that it should be. 15 of the 28 eu member states' parliaments hardly use it at all. there are things we can do here to make the subsidiarity and proportionality checks work. we could take the time parliament has to do that and extend it. we could take and introduce a green card, so that parliaments have the opportunity to put forward their own proposals to the commission which the commission can look at. there is much to be done, and i hope the next report will be even better.
mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur very much for an excellent report. better regulation is a roadmap to a better-functioning single market. better single market rules are not about diminishing workers’ or consumer rights; they are about about diminishing bureaucracy and red tape. the single market rules must be simple, proportionate and applicable in the real economy. we need to move from inadequate to quality impact assessments and to make problem—solving tools, such as solvit and your europe, available to all citizens. dialogue with stakeholders is essential, and so is avoiding gold-plating, where member states impose an extra burden when implementing single market rules. it is therefore bizarre that the left are resisting the better regulation agenda at a time when we need a transparent, effective single market that is closer to our citizens. we need enforcement, not ideological confrontation between business and consumers. as commissioner timmermans rightly says, we need to be big on big things and small on small things.
rapporteur.– madam president, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their excellent cooperation in this report. a special thanks also to the long-distance advisory council, which was very timely in organising a conference in las palmas on the issue so that we could take their input into account in the drafting of the report. i am very happy that we all share the ambition of promoting sustainable fishing, keeping fish stocks above levels that can produce maximum sustainable yield, not only inside the eu waters but also outside the eu. historically there have been very good reasons to be critical of fisheries agreements – they used to be very unsustainable. the great achievement of the new common fisheries policy is the inclusion of rules on fishing activities outside of the eu. the eu is now bound to respect human rights and only fish from surplus stocks. the transparency provisions in the new protocol on mauritania that will soon be voted on at parliament should work as a guideline, and i want to thank the commission here. i know that the negotiations with mauritania were very hard, and the commission should be praised for doing a great job. now we need to make sure that the conditions are met, and we will follow proceedings very closely to make sure that mauritania lives up to its commitments and publishes all its agreements with other states and private companies. there are many good ambitions that we share in this report – the establishment of regulatory framework for joint ventures, for instance. since the cfp is to be a vehicle to provide technical, scientific and financial assistance to third countries, the eu must promote the harmonisation of conditions of access to african waters for all foreign fleets. the regional management of fisheries should be encouraged, including control programmes, and the network of fisheries agreements is to be used to ensure that other distant fishing fleets also respect the same standards of sustainability. the eu should encourage third countries to develop a regulatory framework for joint ventures from the eu and other interests as the best way of ensuring that such joint ventures are sustainable and transparent. over party lines, i am happy that the european parliament shares the objective of using the influence of the eu on the international stage in order to improve fisheries management and transparency standards. the eu should encourage a race to the top, instead of a race to the bottom, in international fisheries. i hope that we can cooperate in the same great spirit on the commission’s new proposal on the fishing authorisation regulation, for which i am rapporteur. we will start at the next strasbourg part-session, as i understand.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the bosnian serb leader radovan karadžić has been convicted by the hague tribunal of, among other things, genocide. the judgement, despite its shortcomings, will be important for establishing the truth about the war and the reconciliation process in bosnia, one of the eu candidate countries still plagued by problems. a few days later, however, extremist vojislav šešelj was acquitted in full, mainly because the tribunal has failed to establish a link between šešelj's hateful rhetoric and the war crimes committed. it may be that it is legally difficult to prove a link between saying, as šešelj said, that one wants to 'dig out the eyes of every croat' and the crimes that have been committed. morally, though, it's not the least bit difficult. if the court cannot condemn these statements, we politicians must do so. the release of šešelj will have major implications for reconciliation and eu enlargement. eu politicians must therefore support both the reconciliation and enlargement process by more clearly condemning the atrocities committed and thereby creating the moral justice that the court has failed to create.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, the report on a holistic approach to migration contains many good elements, and i would like to thank the co-rapporteurs for their excellent work on this. i consider this report an important blueprint for how the eu could create a common well-functioning asylum system fit for today. the crisis in europe demonstrates the complete failure of our national governments to set up a functioning asylum system fit for the world of today, but unfortunately national leaders have played the rhetoric up to such a level that they manage to convince both themselves and parts of the electorate that we cannot do anything about the crisis. we need to get this straight: one million refugees as against 500 million europeans represents 0.2% of our population. compare that to a country that i visited last week, lebanon, where a 30% ratio is facing the everyday life of the lebanese people: there we are talking about a great influx and a big, big problem. i am convinced that the vast majority of european citizens want to see a fair system that grants protection and dignity to people fleeing persecution and war, and at the same time they want member states to share the responsibility in a fair way, where each country gives its contribution and shares the responsibilities. today i hope that we are going to send a clear message to the citizens of europe that this european parliament has the vision for such a system. as the rapporteur for the reform of the dublin regulation, i am especially proud of the joint commitment from all major political groups in this house to real reform of the dublin regulation, which will enable us finally to see a centralised european system where applicants are distributed fairly among eu member states. but after the vote of today, we will all need to get to work to ensure that this initiative is followed by determined and concrete legislation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is just as many have said, namely that we have been working on this report for a very long time now, and we did so even before the large flows of refugees came to us in the autumn. i am not as critical as the previous speaker, but i can also say that this report too has a very strong focus on what is happening in europe rather than on the overall picture of migration and why people are actually moving. however, after the large flows of refugees in the autumn, the debate has largely been about how to deal with the emergency situation, what we call a refugee crisis. at the same time, it is not a refugee crisis that we have, but it is our reception systems that have collapsed, and it is these that we must work with to have a better opportunity in the future. however, i think that perhaps the most important point in this report is the question of humanitarian visas. we have a report in the house. the commission has put forward proposals to amend the visa regulation and parliament will propose the introduction of a humanitarian visa for those who come as refugees. it's a legal and long-term solution to stop people using people smugglers. however, we also need to have a model for those who come as economic migrants. the report also addresses legal avenues for economic migrants. the so-called blue card procedure is not enough. we must find a model for economic migrants other than the highly educated. in this way we can also solve the long-term problem of the need for poor people not to travel by boat across the mediterranean.
mr president, i would like to thank you. how do you think it is even possible to establish a common asylum policy when some countries, with their incredible, irresponsible immigration policies, have gotten their heads in the sand and are begging and pleading for exemption from the proposed forced distribution mechanism that the eu wants to impose on the member states? how can you even believe in a common asylum policy when the limit has long since been reached for many member states, which have taken in more than they can handle? we heard earlier that there was talk of jobs in the member states. i wonder which member states are able to offer jobs to these migrants with little education. in sweden we have very high unemployment, and it's not getting any better. it would therefore be interesting to know which member states have the possibility of offering work. we are talking about solidarity. we are talking about integration. integration is something that everyone talks about a lot, but it rarely works. how can integration even succeed when the member states themselves cannot decide how many migrants they consider they can take in? what is to be done with those member states which reject a common asylum policy? mr president, i would like to thank you. let me repeat once again that the crisis in the eu's agricultural sector affects many people who are active in many different sectors: pigmeat, milk, fruit, vegetables, butter, etc. this requires that we take it seriously and give an adequate response from the eu. i do not agree with those who say that the biggest problem is the conflict with russia and russia's actions. my belief is that the crisis is largely caused by internal problems: inefficient markets, fragmented subsidized sectors that overproduce, lack of innovation, weak competition, falling demand. all of this is met with short-term solutions. the eu commission has so far come up with short-term solutions, such as support for private storage. i understand that such solutions are sometimes necessary. but if the commission is to solve this situation, it must begin to develop measures to address and solve the structural problems of agriculture. until we deal with this, nothing will be better in the long run.
‒ today the european parliament voted on an own-initiative report on the eu's long-term migration policy. the report makes a number of points which i welcome. the emphasis on the common responsibility of member states for migration, the need for legal pathways into europe, secure external borders and the preservation of schengen cooperation as well as a permanent redistribution mechanism. that is why i voted in favour of the final vote. i think, however, that the report mentions the agreement concluded by the eu with turkey in too positive terms. there is every reason to be sceptical of the agreement in question, which was basically only created as a consequence of the lack of a sustainable european migration policy.
in writing. - i've put my vote down. my basic view is that the eu has centralised too much power in its own institutions, especially the bureaucrats in the european commission. national parliaments need much more power within the eu system. i appreciate that the report is critical of the commission and that it stresses that the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality are not properly respected. however, the report also contains unclear texts, particularly on the question of investment protection. with these arguments, and my fundamental criticism of the eu, i choose to abstain.
in writing. - i voted no. the commission has long been talking about simplifying legislation and reducing administrative burdens. this interest has been centred on the companies and their requirements. the result has been that the commission, while talking about simplification and reducing burdens, has put forward proposals which would have made things easier for companies but which would also have led to a deterioration in the working environment, health and social rights of workers. the report does address some of these problems, which is good, but it does not go far enough in welcoming the various new commitments made by the commission. i would like to see the principle established that simplifications must never, under any circumstances, lead to a deterioration of the situation of workers, consumers and the environment. the eu's harmonised legislation also threatens to weaken the original legislation of certain countries in various fields. for these reasons, i choose to vote no.
‒ today the european parliament voted on an own-initiative report on the teaching of eu knowledge in schools. the low level of awareness of eu cooperation among citizens is an urgent problem that should be taken seriously. this is particularly true in sweden, where the eu dimension of the social debate is all but absent. but just because a problem is identified, it does not mean that the eu is the one to solve it. the report calls for eu funds to be used for targeted initiatives to increase eu knowledge among students in the union and provides detailed views on the curricula of the member states. increasing the number of brussels mandates is not the way to achieve greater eu awareness among citizens.
‒ eu citizens show very limited knowledge of the eu and its institutions. of all those surveyed in a survey carried out by eurostat, 44% admitted that they do not know how the eu works. this is very problematic from a democratic point of view, because the eu has a significant impact on our lives. without knowledge of the eu, a common educational plan and a common public media space, the eu will remain a paper structure, overly sensitive to political cycles. the fact that this is happening in a context where the eu is facing several serious crises is even more alarming. eu citizens therefore need to learn much more about the eu, not only in schools but also in political parties, associations, businesses, etc. when garibaldi united italy, the italian patriot massimo d'azeglio said: now that we've created italy, we have to create italians. today, people are tempted to say: now that we have created the eu, we must create europeans before the eu breaks up. i therefore welcome this report.
in writing. - i've lowered my vote. the main objective of the report is to increase mobility and cooperation with the private sector in vocational training as a means of reducing youth unemployment. i think that we should emphasize the importance of expansionary economic policies as a means of tackling unemployment and that education systems should not primarily adapt to the needs of enterprises. on the other hand, the report highlights very important aspects of how erasmus+ can be made more inclusive and also reach groups that are not currently participating in the programmes. it's a self-critical and important reflection. with these arguments, i have decided to abstain.

‒ the eu globalisation fund is intended to provide grants to people who have become unemployed following the closure of european companies. the christian democrats are opposed to this fund for two reasons. first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. i therefore consistently vote against the disbursement of the fund. i sympathize with the volvo workers who have lost their jobs, but it is sweden that should take responsibility for supporting them in the transition phase to a new job not the eu.
in writing. ‒ we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which suggests that the fund does indeed have a negative impact on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its legitimacy and should not be used any more.

in writing. - i voted for the report. this report increases the eu budget for refugees by eur 100 million. there is a great need to improve the eu's work on refugees, from relocation to reception and integration. two million euro will go to europol and efforts to combat terrorism. the left party does not want to increase europol's budget. there is already extensive cooperation between member states, and in addition, bilateral cooperation between national police forces of member states could be extended instead of a super-eu police. the most important thing is that the situation of refugees is significantly improved. that is why i voted in favour of the report.
in writing. ‒ we voted on a non-binding opinion to the commission on the renewal of the authorisation for glyphosate. it was important for me to highlight in particular the fundamental issues surrounding the union's approach to chemicals. i think it is important to stick to the principle that it is not for politicians to make scientific judgments, but that it is something that should be left to science. in europe we have a system where efsa, as a scientific expert, does the scientific analysis on which we then base a possible permit. as the regulatory framework currently stands, there is the possibility of withdrawing a substance's authorisation if scientific facts emerge that lead the scientific analysis to a different conclusion. it is important that this possibility is used and that efsa performs new analyses if new facts become available. as regards glyphosate, i note that efsa has made a recommendation to the commission which enables it to propose a renewal of the authorisation for glyphosate. for me, it is not important how many years a permit is granted, but that we have a system where chemicals can be approved based on a scientific analysis done by experts, not by politicians.
in writing. we support the resolution as it has not been scientifically proven that glyphosate has carcinogenic and endocrine disrupting effects in humans, which is contrary to the precautionary principle. we therefore object to the commission renewing the authorization for glyphosate for another 15 years. in order to avoid the resolution being defeated, we were forced to make certain compromises in the vote.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this resolution because it opens the door to the continued but cautious and responsible authorisation of glyphosate. the european food safety authority (efsa) has reached the firm conclusion that glyphosate, the active substance in plant protection products used in many eu countries, is unlikely to pose a carcinogenic risk. the approval of glyphosate is now extended for seven years and would be re-evaluated in the event that efsa reaches other scientifically based conclusions.
in writing. we welcome the initiative for a new broad strategy for the eu's overall external relations and consider it important that the european parliament, through the report, should be involved and influence its formulation. we are convinced that, among other things, enhanced cooperation with a reformed un, increased european diplomacy and multilateral cooperation are crucial for the eu to effectively respond to current security challenges, such as terrorist attacks. we believe, however, that increased cooperation should not affect the specific nature of the security and defence policy of the member states. we therefore oppose the calls for the development of common defence forces and the formulation of a common defence policy with a european defence union as its objective. we are also critical of the report's call for an increase in the eu's own resources.
in writing. we swedish social democrats welcome a review of the eu's strategy for central asia and believe that the report very clearly emphasises the importance of respect for human rights and democratic principles in the region. we believe that gsp+ can be an important instrument to achieve this and therefore we welcome the application by kyrgyzstan. however, we would like to stress that we believe that the european commission now has a very big responsibility to ensure that kyrgyzstan now fully complies with the gsp+ requirements.
‒ i voted in favour of the resolution on measures to prevent the further spread of the zika virus. the resolution contains a number of important proposals on prevention, coordination of action and planning. i voted against the papers that contained comments on the medical ethics laws of other countries. the european parliament should respect the principle of subsidiarity and the national character of these issues. controversial elements are proposals to require measures in the area of sexual and reproductive health, including abortion. the epp's line was to vote freely on issues relating to sexual and reproductive health and to vote against requests for access to abortion and, if the proposals to not provide access to abortion were rejected, to vote against the resolution as a whole. i voted against the texts on abortion on the basis of the principle of subsidiarity but for the resolution as a whole.
‒ i voted in favour of this resolution. the resolution expresses serious concern at political developments in poland, with a focus on constitutional issues such as amendments to the constitutional court. it is deeply regrettable, however, that not a word is said about the plans to ban abortions altogether. the amendments of the left group, which condemned the restrictions on abortion and defended women's right to their own bodies, were unfortunately not passed. the resolution would, of course, have been much better by showing that oppression of women and contempt for democracy are closely linked. while the other political groups are silent, we in the left group stand in solidarity with all the thousands of demonstrators who have protested in poland and elsewhere in europe for democracy and women's rights. that is why i voted in favour of both the amendments and the resolution.
in writing. ‒ there are worrying developments in poland, where the new government is putting forward proposals that challenge the values and principles that are the foundation of a democratic state in europe. this resolution focuses on the importance of all member states upholding the principles which are the foundation of democracy in europe. it is therefore worrying that the polish government, for example, is challenging the rule of law by not following the ruling of the polish constitutional court. it was important to have a strong support from parliament in this vote. as for the question of abortion rights, it is something that i and the centre party stand for in all situations. our position on this matter has been clear for a long time. this resolution, which was raised precisely because of the polish government's decision not to comply with its constitutional court, is not primarily about the right to abortion but about fundamental constitutional issues. i therefore chose not to vote on two amendments tabled by the gue/ngl group, as they were not in line with the resolution's actual focus.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to express my warm thanks to the rapporteurs, as do all the others, for their tireless work on this legislative package. i am particularly pleased today that we now have the opportunity to vote on legislation on data protection which will protect the fundamental rights and freedoms of citizens while not creating too many difficulties for those who have to deal with the new regulatory framework in different ways. my main objection initially was the need to protect the possibility of register-based research, particularly in my home country sweden, but also in some other member states. the vast research databases that have been built up over a very long period of time are an invaluable resource for scientists from all over the world in their search for treatments and drugs to combat disease and ill health of all kinds. it would have been a disaster if this research had been made impossible. it is therefore very gratifying that the scientists who have raised their concerns have been heard, and we have a balanced legislative proposal which i am happy and proud to be able to adopt now. madam president, i am afraid i am not here to sing with the angels. after so many years – let’s be honest – we did not achieve the aim. we wanted simple harmonised rules and legal certainty, with the right balance between protecting individuals and the free flow of data. i am glad we have achieved one pillar – integrity – but in europe we are not building common rules on exceptions. the result, i am afraid, is fragmentation, legal uncertainty and complexity. we wanted to provide a legal playing field, reducing red tape and boosting the single market. but where is the big success when smes are warning us that there will be more red tape, less investments and less jobs in europe? our hope was to pave the way for the digital single market by removing borders, barriers and burdens, but these rules are not future-proof and could actually be a break to innovation. i also regret that we missed a big opportunity to protect our children online, and i hope we will be able to find a solution with the digital community.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when one hears the representatives of the governments of the member states, one easily gets the impression that if only the pnrs had existed, terrorism and serious crime would be a thing of the past. the reality, however, is not so simple. the pnr is one of many tools that can facilitate law enforcement action, particularly in the area of serious crime and terrorism. we would have achieved real efficiency if we had deepened cooperation, if we had had an automatic exchange of information, which the governments of the member states unfortunately did not want to see. after all, the pnr is a tool that can help protect and instill security in citizens across the eu. that is why i and a large majority of my group will vote in favour of the pnr.
madam president, to face the magnitude of the threat against us, we should unite and be constructive to reassure citizens that we are doing all we can for their security. how much more blood has to be shed before we come together and drop this ideologically infected debate? i am sad that there is still resistance to adopting obvious measures when the experts tell us clearly that pnr is essential to prevent and combat terrorism. passenger name records are not a magic recipe against terrorism but an essential tool in a limited toolbox. let us be honest: eu common rules on pnr will protect citizens’ privacy more than the current national system, not less. at the moment we do not know what happens to our data when we travel. with the new pnr we will have more transparency and common safeguards. member states will be able to exchange information in a secure way, also via europol. the key is better information-sharing to identify the routes taken by foreign fighters going back and forth, for example to syria and iraq, to train and plan attacks against us. i also welcome the fact that the member states decided – despite the fact that the epp was against it – to also include intra—eu flights. we also need to work on anti—radicalisation, prevention, better control of our external border and more cooperation with third countries. but the key is that we come together to face terrorism. for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. i wish i could stand here today and applaud the developments in turkey. i wish i could say that democratic development is on the right track. i wish i could say that freedom of the press, freedom of expression, the independence of the judiciary and human rights are generally respected. i wish i could say that turkey's path to eu membership is without stumbling blocks. i wish i could say that the peace talks with the kurds are going well and that peace is in sight. i'm afraid i can't say anything like that. most of it is going in the wrong direction. it is a tragedy, especially for all the democrats in turkey who, against all odds, stand up for democracy, human rights for all, peace and justice. this year's resolution is highly critical of developments in turkey in terms of virtually all rights. it also calls very clearly on both sides to cease violence and return to peace negotiations. however, to negotiate, you must have a party to talk to. today, one side is building palaces, while the other has been imprisoned and isolated since 1999. abdullah öcalan has not seen his family since october 2015 and has not seen his lawyer since july 2011. his correspondence has been completely blocked since april 2015. according to several un envoys, it constitutes cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment that may amount to torture. that's why we greens are proposing that the isolation of öcalan be lifted.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the report on turkey contains many facts which call into question the country's current leadership, not to mention its suitability as a candidate country. the report shows that turkey's positive development has stalled, particularly in the areas of fundamental democratic aspects such as the rule of law, freedom of expression and freedom of assembly. we can read how the turkish government has created a constitution which stipulates a parliamentary lock-up of as much as 10 per cent. this shows that turkey is a country with clear authoritarian tendencies. it is also clear that turkey is playing a very high and risky game in its foreign and security policy. in its eagerness to revive old ottoman ambitions for great power, it has plunged itself into the deadly intrigues of the middle east. turkey has been involved in the conflict in syria, and many even claim - whether justified or not - that turkey is playing under the table with murderous groups like al-nusra and isis. as the icing on the cake, turkey has succeeded in pushing for highly favourable agreements with the eu on the issue of migration.

in writing. we, the swedish social democrats in the european parliament, voted in favour of the report on a directive to protect undisclosed trade secrets from being unlawfully acquired, exploited and disclosed. we have been critical of the commission's original proposals from the start, but have managed to negotiate documents calling for exemptions for whistleblowers and freedom of expression and press. we also succeeded in securing wording on the right to collective bargaining, worker mobility and reporting to authorities. these demands, which parliament and the council have agreed to, are too important for us to jeopardise at second reading when the likelihood of a majority is deemed to be non-existent. furthermore, we consider that the definition of trade secrets is still too broad and may leave room for different interpretations. but given the guarantees we have managed to achieve in other important parts of the legislative text, we consider it unjustified to vote against the proposal as a whole.
‒ the report sets out parliament's overall orientations for its budget for the financial year 2017. i have voted against the whole report because the drafting process has been very flawed and because i do not think it is appropriate for parliament to adopt such a detailed position before the ordinary budgetary procedure has even started. i have chosen to support the principle of parliament moving all its work to one place, i.e. no more moving to strasbourg 12 times a year. i voted against the parliament's increase in the budget for next year. i have also voted against some very detailed reports on travel allowances and so on because i do not think it is relevant to deal with that kind of detailed questions.
