in writing. ‒ the ongoing energy transition is moving away from an energy system based on traditional centralised power generation towards a more decentralised, energy efficient and flexible energy system based largely on renewable energy. in this transition, it is becoming increasingly important to empower energy consumers and i welcome the report in these areas as the eu has an important role to play here. unfortunately, however, the report contains a lot of information on energy poverty, which does not belong at eu level, since this is social policy that the eu should not be involved in, and i therefore voted against those parts.
in writing. we social democrats have voted in favour of the report on a new gift for energy consumers. we have abstained on a ban on home selling because we believe that global measures rather than individual bans are needed to tackle the consumer problems of home selling. we also voted on a paper on working towards targeted electricity discounts for certain groups. we are convinced that active action will be required to combat energy poverty. discounts to different groups may be one way, and where energy companies choose to give such a discount we are of course not against it. however, we believe that there are more long-term sustainable measures than electricity discounts, such as an active distribution policy and targeted energy efficiency initiatives, etc., to reduce the energy costs of vulnerable groups. we therefore do not want to confine ourselves to this particular measure.
‒ on the report on a new gift for energy consumers, we voted against the demand that energy poverty be tackled at european level. energy poverty needs to be addressed, but it should be done within the social safety net of each member state and is the responsibility of each member state. better prices and conditions for consumers require market opening, the phase-out of subsidies and a balance between supply and demand. it should be the focus of the construction of the european energy union.
‒ today the european parliament voted on an own-initiative report by the committee on gender equality on women's poverty in europe. it is important to highlight poverty from a gender perspective, as women are over-represented among europeans still living in material poverty, although it is important to emphasise that progress has been made in the right direction. unfortunately, the present report proposes incorrect and destructive solutions, both in substance and from the point of view of subsidiarity. the report calls for minimum wages, the enforcement of the now-repealed maternity leave directive and the eu budget to be increased to directly subsidize various activities deemed to be in favour of equality. the report also contains a general and general criticism of the market economy. i therefore chose to vote no.
in writing. ‒ this report is about poor women in the eu. the subject is very important and i have voted in several rounds in favour of reports and legislative proposals aimed at strengthening the position of vulnerable women. however, there were some points in this report which i found very problematic and which were also voted on in plenary. i therefore had no opportunity to vote in favour of the report as a whole. this was particularly the case with letters rejecting the eu's austerity policy and calling on the commission to reinstate the maternity leave directive and legislate on european parental leave. the first i do not agree with and the second i think are definitely not issues to be pursued at eu level. i am in favour of very generous parental leave and have pushed these issues hard in sweden, but it is opening up a development that i do not want to see to start legislating on this kind of issues at eu level.
in writing. - i voted no. non-tariff barriers to trade in the internal market can be understood in many ways. there are unnecessary administrative rules and bureaucratic procedures in several countries which make trade difficult; of course, such obstacles must be overcome. however, i have always been in favour of the right of countries to develop environmental and consumer laws to better protect people, nature and animals. it should not be seen as a barrier to trade, but as a political example. it should also be clear that the labour law and collective agreements of a country should never be called into question by virtue of the rules of the internal market. but unfortunately, the eu's prioritization of trade within the single market has had negative consequences in several countries for the environment, consumers and animal rights, as well as for labour law and collective agreements. the report is not clear enough on issues such as these, which is why i am voting against.
‒ a report responding to the commission's strategy for developing the internal market. a developed and better functioning internal market is a key factor in the recovery of europe's economies, and the report contains many important points. i have voted in favour of the report as a whole. parts of the report highlight a general requirement for 'made in' for industrial products. i believe that this would disrupt the functioning of the internal market too much and i have therefore voted against these points.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted today in favour of the own-initiative report on the 'single market strategy'. it is important that the european parliament signals to the commission our priorities in the internal market, which is an essential part of our cooperation for, among other things, job creation. since the internal market must be accompanied by effective protection for consumers, workers and the environment, we chose to vote against a few points where we consider the texts to be contrary to our values. we believe that the member states should of course continue to be able to legislate on the protection of consumers, workers and the environment without this being considered an unjustified obstacle to the internal market. we also voted against the proposals which oppose municipal self-government in terms of urban planning. the swedish social democratic delegation voted in favour of several of the good papers which highlighted the importance of strengthening consumer protection, workers' rights and environmental requirements. we also fully support the papers which highlight the potential of the social economy to create sustainable and secure jobs within the eu's internal market. we were therefore able to support the report in the final vote, as there were enough good papers and balanced the text.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the social stigma and sometimes even legal discrimination faced by people living with hiv in health care, workplaces and education is still widespread and causes great psychological suffering for many. discriminatory laws and policies on people living with hiv must end and adequate information on hiv must reach the wider community. hiv prevention is a shared responsibility and should be addressed to all groups in society. the biggest risk to the spread of hiv is precisely the stigma that leads many to not find out the information or dare to seek treatment. in the fight against and prevention of hiv, therefore, human rights need to be at the centre and serious efforts should be made to combat stigma and discrimination against people living with hiv. last year, who recorded the highest number of new hiv infections in europe since the 1980s: 142,000. in the eu, about two million are infected. in the eu, we need politicians at national and local level to take the fight against hiv. we need more investment in health care, more money for prevention, more needle exchange programmes and, of course, more aid to third countries.
mr president, i would like to thank you. belarus' first two nuclear power plants are being built right now, and these reactors are being built with the support of russia, the russian company, even though they will be building the nuclear power plant in its entirety. in addition, russia will invest the equivalent of sek 1,5 billion in the first stage of the construction of the plant. all this comes at a time when belarus is already very dependent on russia. 90% of belarus' energy imports come from a single importer, namely russia. once again, we see russia trying to use energy as a weapon to influence other countries in the direction that suits the kremlin. this has happened before in armenia, kyrgyzstan, kazakhstan, estonia, latvia, lithuania, poland, ukraine, georgia and it will happen again tomorrow in belarus. the european parliament must therefore examine this project. firstly, because the project obviously has a huge environmental impact on other countries, and secondly, because the project is not in line with the eu's objective of diversifying europe's energy sources. the commission must stop pretending to be nothing and consider the geostrategic implications of such a project.
madam president, we need efficient tools to deal with imbalances in the food sector in order to ensure fairness, transparency and trust. the supply chain initiative is a good process. it is gaining ground. now we need to show concrete results on impartial governance, confidential claims and sanctions. it is about creating a culture of compliance in the member states. and the solution is enforcing rules, such as the late payment directive, rather than creating new rules which could burden smes even more. competition law is a key, in food retail as elsewhere, to ensuring a level playing field. the commission has been clear that, on the basis of independent study and years of work together with us, eu legislation unfortunately does not solve the problem. colleagues, i call on you to vote against eu legislation on unfair trading practices. let us not give our dear farmers, smes and consumers, who have legitimate concerns, the illusion of a quick fix from brussels.
madam president, innovation remains a key priority for meeting the biggest challenges faced by farming today. in the context of climate change, rising food demand, population growth and threats to the environment, we really need to support agricultural innovation in order to increase the productivity and sustainability of the sector. in other words, farmers will need to produce more with less in the future. however, adoption of the new agricultural technologies by european farms has been modest until now. the challenge before us, commissioner, is therefore to support the transfer of knowledge from researchers to farmers and to make use of innovative techniques by updating the relevant legislation, as well as the creation of new financing opportunities for farmers. most of the farms of today have little in common with the farms of the 1950s and, if we get it right, the farms of 2050 will probably differ significantly from the farms of today.
madam president, the two countries in which modern slavery is most widespread today are uzbekistan and pakistan. these are two countries with which the eu has close relations. moreover, the report of the international labour organization stated the crucial importance for the authorities of uzbekistan to continue with measures to keep children away from the cotton harvest, as child labour in cotton harvesting has been widely used before. the eu parliament has constantly condemned the systematic violation of labour rights in uzbekistan and pakistan. the commission and the member states have looked away even though forced labour, both adults and children, is a common practice in both countries. the commission should firmly and resolutely address such cases of human rights violations in these countries and step up the fight against forced labour in central asia.
madam president, in the european union the tax load is 10% to 15% higher than in any other competing region in the world. this means that there is no other place in the world where companies and employees are paying as much tax as here. let us not forget that when we talk about these issues. this is not – and must not be – about fighting european business and european companies. it must not be about increasing taxes. it must be about addressing those who are bending the rules and those who are breaking the rules. it must be very clear there is a responsibility for national authorities, in cooperation with each other, to minimise all of these things that are undermining taxation. but it must also lead to the fact that we stay within the oecd rules because, if we go beyond them, we are creating a disadvantage for european industry and are also creating new loopholes and new grey zones for those who would like to misuse the different systems. so we need to ensure that investments can come to europe and we need to have a stable legislation and responsibility for national authorities.
mr president, i would like to thank you. tax evasion and aggressive tax planning arouse the emotions of citizens because they undermine the common good to which everyone contributes. of course, this also has a negative impact on the internal market and we must now ensure that business, for both large and small players, can take place on equal terms throughout the eu. critical scrutiny is essential for a functioning democracy and citizens must be informed, because then they can also demand that we make available political decisions that will make scandals such as the 'luxleaks' and the 'panama papers' effectively impossible in the future. the citizens expect this from us. public and critical scrutiny is good, also in relation to national tax systems. i can agree with the rapporteur and regret that the commission has not yet put forward a legislative proposal on the consolidated corporate tax base, because this proposal has the support of business people and would simplify the tax system in the internal market and ensure that we create a clearer common playing field for all actors throughout the eu. and we need that.
in writing. - i voted yes to the report. i am doing this only because it is a logical consequence of croatia's accession to the eu, which means that it will also join the eu's existing trade agreements. however, i do not think that the eu-colombia/peru trade agreement is a good agreement, which is a view that i share with many trade union and popular movements in colombia. my party voted against the agreement when it was discussed in the european parliament. however, this vote is not about the agreement itself, but about the consequences of croatia's accession to the eu. that's the only reason i voted yes.
‒ today the european parliament voted on a report on the need for a coherent policy for international development cooperation. evaluating and optimising policy tools to create a well-thought-out and consistent policy is important. this is being handled very well and in the final vote i voted for the report. at the same time, i consider that certain sections of the report are problematic, which i have highlighted in separate votes. this is particularly the case with the proposal to introduce transaction taxes to finance development.
‒ this report highlights the importance of all eu policies contributing to the achievement of the union's development policy objectives and to the achievement of the international sustainable development goals. unfortunately, the report highlights things which, in my view, should not be seen as a problem for international development. i think it is wrong that the report highlights free trade as a problem for poverty reduction and highlights, for example, an international tax on financial transactions as a positive alternative. i voted against those negative aspects.
‒ this report highlights the importance of investment and development in farming in the context of international development work. it must also be directed at smes and help to strengthen entrepreneurship. unfortunately, the report also contains anti-free trade language and affirms that protectionist measures should be allowed. unfortunately, it also contains writings which are sceptical and in some respects hostile to new agricultural technologies. i voted against such writings as far as possible.
in writing. ‒ today we are voting on the new alliance for food and nutrition security in sub-saharan africa. millions of people are still hungry in southern africa, and malnutrition is a contributing cause of more than half of all child deaths worldwide. it's something we have to take very seriously. that is why the new initiative to reduce hunger based on the asian model is often called the 'green revolution'. we cannot support the criticism of the european parliament of the initiative or the demand that the whole of southern africa should become a test-bed for the small-scale, non-mechanized, organic and gmo-free farming that the green party usually advocates in sweden. it risks starving millions of people unnecessarily. those who now want to deny african agriculture modern technology and modern methods are also responsible for the hunger and misery that such a policy will inevitably cement millions of poor people into. that is why we are voting against the report.
‒ i chose to vote against some controversial points, not least because of my conviction that trade creates prosperity and that the removal of trade barriers is therefore a good thing. as regards the other points, i agree with the report and therefore voted in favour.
in writing. – the greens/efa group support many of the issues raised in the van orden report: the need to increase member states’ contributions to un peacekeeping and the clear language on the issue of sexual exploitation and abuses by personnel in peacekeeping. however, the greens/efa group opted for abstention at the final vote since because of the language we oppose paragraph 15, which states that ‘oda needs to be redesigned under the oecd framework through peacebuilding lenses’ and that the edf regulation ‘should be reviewed in order to allow programming design that includes peace, security and justice expenditures that have development-related motivation’. we believe that current development aid already allows good governance projects in the security sector and there are dedicated eu instruments for security sector reform and that such a change would limit the impact of development aid. the last sentence of paragraph 20 calls for a legal base for transferring military equipment to third countries financed by the eu budget. we believe that this is illegal as article 41(2) clearly states that expenditure arising from military or defence operations (e.g. military training missions) cannot be charged to the eu budget. paragraph 21 supports the council position favouring eu budget financing of military equipment for third states.
‒ i voted in favour of this report because i am convinced that we need legislation to restore balance and guarantee fairness and transparency in the food chain. it is therefore important to increase the bargaining power of farmers by improving producer organisation and cooperation throughout the food chain.
in writing. ‒ i share the view expressed in this report that we must increase agricultural productivity in a sustainable way. this requires more extensive cooperation between scientists and farmers, and stronger and more successful transfer of knowledge to end-users. this also requires a more innovation-friendly eu regulatory framework.
mr president, i support the parliament’s call for modernising european agriculture. it is the best way to gain competitiveness on global markets. i also welcome that we have put food waste back on parliament’s agenda. efficient farming is important to feed an ever—growing population in the world, but it is also important to use what we produce. 100 million tons of food is lost in the eu every year and food waste is economically, sustainably and ethically unacceptable. my campaign ‘basta to food waste’ has highlighted that it is high time to step up our action over decades. we need to take the lead as opinion builders and work with all parts of the food chain: producers, retailers and consumers. each of us must take our shared responsibility and change our lifestyles and attitude to food. at eu level, we need common definition, better data collection and targets, and to raise awareness, exchange best practices and clarify expiry dates and best before labels and rules for charity. i will finish by saying that food waste is our common challenge and we need not only to consider it an option, but a necessity.
‒ i voted in favour of this report because i believe that we need to support innovation in the agricultural sector to increase productivity and sustainability. in view of the increasing demand for food resulting from global population growth and some of the negative effects that agriculture can have on the climate, constructive measures need to be taken to enable farmers to produce more food, with fewer resources and in a more climate-friendly way.
madam president, there is no doubt that we must cooperate better with third countries in our neighbourhood in order to manage the root causes of migration, to ensure safe and legal routes to europe as well as a dignified returns policy for those that are not entitled to international protection. i agree with the commissioner on this, but we must get our act together and manage migration flows. just deporting everybody to so-called third countries is a dead end. it is a no-go, and the commission must realise that the eu-turkey statement, or deal, or whatever status it has, is a dead end. it is a no-go created to circumvent the democratic scrutiny of this house. let us instead now focus on the creation of a dignified european asylum system assisting our neighbourhood but never, never, outsource european responsibility to third countries, otherwise this continent, this union, will be held hostage indefinitely.
madam president, i would like to thank you. this proposal is not about cooperation. it's not about migration either. it is a question of stopping refugees, of mobilizing all possible means to this end - economic, trade, visa and even military. do you not see for yourselves how dangerous, inhuman and future-threatening this development is? the eu-turkey agreement is a disgraceful agreement that violates international law and human rights, and now you want to continue on the same track. further externalising and closing the eu's borders through similar agreements between countries such as eritrea and sudan. it's a dead end. the citizens of the eu do not expect a cynically closed europe where we can hardly breathe any longer. we expect a serious discussion based on how we can organise a more solidary and dignified reception and take greater responsibility for the reception of refugees in europe. back to the drawing board!

madam president, i would like to thank you. we must remember that every human being is, has been or can, if life so chooses, be forced to become a migrant. and thanks to the technological advances that we're seeing and thanks to the era of globalization that we're living in, it's easier for people to move around the globe. this, of course, places great demands on our societies to manage the flow of migration. whether it's asylum or legal migration, we need to do a better job of integrating those who have arrived. anything else is a waste of people. with their skills, with their knowledge, experience, ambition and willingness to contribute. unfortunately, we have done this in many countries, including sweden, where it has taken nearly seven years for a person to become self-sufficient and live on the income of his own work. it's not acceptable anymore. but integration, i think, has to be built from the ground up, built locally. it can't succeed because it's a decree from above. on the other hand, successful integration can be built on the sharing of experiences, both good and bad, between member states. learning from each other, for an integration that can lead to increased growth, to innovation and creativity. we need that across the eu. migration and successful integration. it can be a new life, new opportunities, new successes. and we need that, so we really need to be able to do this with deep integration.

madam president, i welcome the action plan to improve member states’ integration of migrants and the revision of the blue card directive. the time has come to change our rhetoric and recognise that europe needs workers to sustain our economy and our welfare. we have to move from crisis response to managing flows according to our needs. for this, the eu—turkey action plan is a crucial way to have an effective migration policy. we need to save and protect people fleeing conflict, but not all migrants are refugees. those who do not need international protection must be returned safely. developing tools for legal migration to europe is also a way to combat irregular migration. it is a way to prevent people from risking their lives at sea in the hands of criminals who exploit them. i call on member states to fully implement the labour migration tools that exist: the seasonal workers, the intra-corporate transferees and the students and researchers directives. skill development and access to the labour market are the highway to integration, and early integration is key to effective integration. the sooner children go to school and migrants work and contribute to our society, the sooner they will feel part of our society and respect our system and values. this is why it is also important that refugees – in particular women – have access to work to ensure empowerment and social inclusion. in the global fight for talents, europe needs common, clear and simple rules to attract skilled workers and match the needs of our economies. it is not about migrants taking our jobs, but it is about migrants contributing to new... madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the commission for this skills agenda. there has been a long-term effort to make qualifications more comparable between member states, and this is of enormous importance. it is also an important new complement to reaching all those adults who are not eligible for the youth guarantee but who are without education. this is a group that is very weak in the labour market and it is precisely them who need our support. i would, however, like to raise a little finger of caution to the busyness of entrepreneurship that permeates communication. entrepreneurship is described as the way people deal with a labour market and society that is changing very rapidly and to deal with complexity and uncertainty. i think we can all agree that entrepreneurship is important, but to say that it's the solution to all the problems that are being described is going too far. in the worst case, entrepreneurship may be called 'self-employment'.
madam president, there is a swedish expression which says that you should not shout hello until you are over the river. i do not know how you look upon that in other parts of europe, but i would like to advise that as an approach from the commission during the review, because the fact that the money in efsi is spent and used is not a success as such. it would have been a surprise if not, because public—subsidised and public—guaranteed money is always preferred to other types. the remaining problem is that europe still has an investment gap of between eur 300 to 400 to 500 billion annually, and we see it very clearly when we look at the flows of foreign direct investments. since 2007, they have been more or less halved, while in other competing regions they have been doubled. this is the core problem, and in the review the commission needs to look at whether this money is replacing other investments. is it creating attraction to more investments, and at what does it mean that we have undermined research science and the connecting europe facility? this must not be an alibi for reforms to make europe the most entrepreneurial economy in the world by attracting investments, instead of promoting them. blue-card answer. – i think the answer is both simple and complex, as always. the main reason for investments in any of our countries is that you can get a return on the investment. otherwise, it is not an investment. therefore, we need to strive for stable public finances but also remind ourselves that, if we in the european union on average have a 10% to 15% higher tax load than in any other competing region, of course this is influencing the climate for investment. so let us change the climate for investment in all parts of the european union and go ahead with that.

in writing. - of course, tax evasion is completely unacceptable. to reduce sophisticated tax planning and to counter tax evasion, we need globally coherent rules to close the loopholes created by fragmented systems. the issue of legislation and regulation is bigger than a single country, a single region or a single continent. common international guidelines are a prerequisite for tackling the problems. the common frameworks developed by the oecd through beps are a key element in the work on tax rules to combat tax base erosion and tax evasion. in the process, it is important to ensure that harmonised rules allow for circumstances specific to national tax systems. it is crucial that individual countries or the eu do not deviate from the common guidelines already drawn up by means of directives. we risk again being left with a patchwork of regulations which in turn open up new forms of tax planning. it is important to ensure equal treatment of enterprises irrespective of their size. if we are to succeed in this work, we must succeed globally. we cannot then add more rules at eu level, as this report does.

in writing. we swedish social democrats share the view that the space sector is playing an increasingly important role in security policy. space research and increased space capacity can be very useful from a broad security perspective, for example by facilitating humanitarian operations and disaster management, or for monitoring global warming and natural disasters such as droughts, earthquakes or floods. we want to see cooperation to safeguard access to space and the use of space services for peaceful purposes so that they benefit human development and security. however, we would like to stress how important we think it is that space activities are carried out with due care and that the eu remains firm in its commitment not to militarize space. we also do not want a common defence union to be established in the future and therefore voted against these texts in the report.

for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. getting elected in turkey is not only difficult because of the high threshold of 10 percent it can also be dangerous. you can go to jail for your opinions. despite the threshold, the hdp, which brings together various minority groups - the greens and a number of democratic groups - managed to gain a large margin in the turkish parliament in june last year, but also later in the new elections, although with fewer seats and after a dirty election campaign. in its reply to the commission's progress report on turkey, parliament said of the elections: 'parliament condemned ... the threats and harassment of the media and the discrimination against opposition parties in the pre-election reporting, the atmosphere of violence and threats characterised mainly by the attacks on individual candidates and the offices of opposition parties, in particular the peoples' democratic party (hdp), and the strong political polarisation.' on 20 may this year, the turkish parliament approved a proposal to allow the prosecution and punishment of members of parliament. it is mainly directed against the hdp and it is likely to be mainly about violations of the turkish terrorism legislation. it is the same law that is the great stumbling block when we discuss visa facilitation for turkey, the law that is so markedly different from the way we in the eu view terrorism, freedom of expression, freedom of assembly, freedom of the press, and which i wonder how many of us who have openly criticized the turkish president would suffer if we were turkish citizens living in turkey. how many of us would have signed the call for academics? but even those accused of corruption get their immunity lifted. why can't the same be said for members of the government or the president himself? the issue of corruption is another of the stumbling blocks for turkey to meet the criteria for visa facilitation. the european parliament has also expressed its concern in this report. so this is how we write it. the european parliament reiterates its concern about the anti-terrorism law. in particular, its broad and extremely vague definition of terrorism, organised crime and propaganda makes it impossible to determine the exact nature of these crimes. we also write: deplores the distortion of the course of justice in favour of certain politicians, which became a daily occurrence in turkey after the 2013 corruption scandal; the president wants to change the constitution and give us more power. the president wants to tighten the anti-terrorism law even further. the victims on the way are parliamentarians, academics, journalists, judges, minorities and all those who believe in democracy and the rule of law and those who believe that dialogue is better than violence to solve problems. president erdoğan has now signed the law. he should be ashamed of himself.
mr president, i must say as the rapporteur for trade defence instruments, it has been pretty bizarre to have a debate on a file that was presented in april 2013 and that parliament took a clear position on in february 2014. two commissioners have tried to reform this and both, peter mandelson and karel de gucht, failed because of the inability of council to agree. but now we do not have time for that anymore. by 11 december the council has to find a solution, a robust system that is legally compatible with the wto, in order to not end up in a trade conflict. you have to find a balance between the producer industries’ competitiveness and the user industries’ competitiveness, and the interests of the consumers. but there are many myths when it comes to trade defence instruments. the most predominant one is probably that they do not deliver any protection at all and do not work to protect industries. but let us look at steel. we have 20 measures today in steel. measures that have delivered rapid, sustained and substantial degrees of import to the european union, from 85 to 99%. and we know that, as mr frédéric bastiat concluded 200 years ago when he wrote the candlemaker’s petition, many industries are willing to go to considerable extremes to get even more protection. therefore, we, as lawmakers, have to take the responsibility to guarantee that we strike a balance in the broadest public interest, and that is what i expect from you.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. whaling is incredibly cruel. whales are harpooned and met with a slow death. in modern whaling, the harpoon is fired at the whale with a grenade. the shell explodes a foot into the whale's body, sending it into shock until it dies. since 1986, there has been a ban on commercial whaling. the goal is to save whales from extinction. the only exceptions to the ban are hunting for indigenous needs and for scientific purposes. when the decision was made, suddenly japan became very interested in doing research. they started their whale research program just one year after the moratorium and continued to hunt whales in antarctica. since 1987, japan has killed over 13,000 whales in its so-called scientific programs, even though it was widely known that its whaling was primarily for commercial purposes. however, in march 2014, the international court of justice in the hague ruled that japanese whaling was not conducted for scientific purposes. i am very disappointed with japan, which, despite this ruling, has resumed whaling. they now plan to catch 4,000 whales over a 12-year period. so far this season, they have killed 333 humpbacks, more than 200 of which were pregnant, several with twins. japan does not respect international law. we must make it clear to japan that this is not acceptable. it is not only contrary to international law, but it poses a serious threat to biodiversity and marine ecosystems. in addition, demand for whale meat has fallen sharply as japanese consumers have become better informed. this makes it all the more incomprehensible why japan insists on continuing. the eu must respond. it is time for the council to put more pressure on japan and not to hesitate to take legal action if necessary. the eu must use the means of pressure at its disposal - political and economic - to bring this cruel slaughter to an end.
‒ today we voted in favour of the resolution on the competitiveness of subcontractors in the railway sector. the resolution highlighted, inter alia, the need for future trade agreements and ongoing negotiations with the us, china and japan not to lead to outsourcing and deindustrialisation in the eu. this means that we believe that agreements should be based on free trade, open markets and competition, which is also the basis for europe's economy and rail sector to grow and be competitive in a global market.

for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to begin by thanking mr wałęsa and his team for the work they have put into completing the negotiations. the baltic sea plan is the first management plan in which parliament and the council of ministers jointly set a limit on the amount we can fish. the negotiations have been tough and i am disappointed that we did not go all the way to defend parliament's ambitious position which would have meant an end to overfishing within the eu. an ocean is not an aquarium where all the factors can be controlled. the situation in our sensitive baltic inland waters is also extremely difficult. this uncertainty requires safety margins when determining maximum sustainable yields of fish. to achieve the goal of a healthy baltic sea with viable fish stocks, we cannot always fish at the maximum sustainable yield - fmsy. we need to stay below the surface so that the fish stocks can recover. instead of deciding how much we should fish under fmsy, the negotiations became about what situations you can fish over fmsy and how much over fmsy. i regret that i cannot recommend to my group that we vote in favour of this agreement. it is not in line with the objective set out in the basic regulation of the eu fisheries policy. at least we managed to incorporate a number of protective mechanisms into the plan. the first test is that three weeks ago there was an alarming report on the state of the cod in the western baltic sea, which is threatened with collapse because of poor rejuvenation. the ball is now in commissioner vella's court. the commission will now draw up a proposal for new fishing quotas based on the baltic sea plan. in order to protect the cod in the western stock, i expect the commission to propose quotas which are in line with the conservation measures described in the plan. we have every reason to be concerned about stopping the overfishing of european waters, and from a citizen's perspective it is important that the eu institutions respect and follow the decisions we take and not undermine them by pushing through conflicting decisions. to say that we are going to protect our oceans from overfishing and at the same time allow it is hypocrisy. it undermines, if anything, confidence in politics and european cooperation.

mr president, i would like to thank you. tackling energy and climate issues is one of the most important things we have to do in this mandate. we must make real what we promised each other in paris, and then we must ensure that we phase out subsidies to fossil fuels and instead focus policy on promoting a transition to 100 per cent renewable energy. one way of doing this is to strengthen the interconnection between our different energy systems in europe. we greens want to remove unnecessary bureaucratic obstacles that prevent this and make large, strong investments to achieve our interconnection goals. and we emphasize that increased regional cooperation can increase the cost-effectiveness of the systems and help that when the sun does not shine in one country, we can use the energy from wind power in another country or water power in a third. renewable energy requires a more decentralized system, and we need to do more to create it now.
mr president, energy efficiency is about economic efficiency and reducing emissions, not about reducing our economies. i think it is important to remember that, because sometimes in these discussions it sounds as if we think that just by setting a target then we will solve the problem. that is not the case and that belief would undermine our ability to reach our objective. the magnitude of change lies of course in reforming our markets in order to make them open and secure so that we get new generations of technology. i think there are some key issues that we need to think about. first of all, open markets – open for new and renewable energies, but also open for competition, pressing prices, but also securing the best way of using the energy we have. market prices, because otherwise there will be no opportunity for the new emerging energy sources to be successful. we will need to secure economic growth to invest in new generations of technology, and we need to rely also on nuclear power. but our watchword is, of course, less fossil fuels and less heavy infrastructure investments that make, for example, natural gas cheaper than it really is, especially when it comes from monopolies. so the whole issue, mr commissioner, about nordstream 2 is also part of achieving energy efficiency in the european union and a functioning energy union.

‒ today we voted in favour of the report on the state of play of renewable energy. renewable energy is a prerequisite for achieving environmental and climate objectives and requires an energy market based on open and free competition, market prices and incentives to invest in new technologies and transmission. the report adopted by parliament proposes a binding target of at least 30% renewable energy with national sub-targets. however, we believe that it is, and should remain, the exclusive competence of the member states to control their own energy mix. the report also includes a paragraph which points to bioenergy from forestry as potentially harmful to the environment and the climate. sweden has a long and broad experience of using forests in an efficient and environmentally friendly way, which uses forest resources with the least possible climate and environmental impact. we therefore do not share the report's problematic approach, but on the contrary, we see sustainable forestry as part of the effort to reduce emissions.

mr president, i would like to thank you. let me give you a different and somewhat less distorted picture of trade than the one we have just heard from the greens. in 1990, world trade accounted for 19 percent of world gdp. it was a total value of $3.5 trillion. today, it represents 30 percent of the world's gdp, and its total value has more than quadrupled. at that time, 38 percent of the world's population lived in absolute poverty. today, 10 percent of the world's population lives in absolute poverty. so it's been halved twice, but it's not just that there's an absolute and causal relationship there. the trade in a wealthy (unheard of word) eradicates poverty. the problem we have is that this development is being dampened. the increase in trade today and since 2008 is less than half the average since 1990. and this is unfortunately very much due to protectionism. since 2008, when almost 3800 protectionist measures were introduced worldwide, only one third of the trade facilitation measures have been implemented. from 1995 to 2008, the average tariff was halved from 19% to 10% but since then it has remained at almost the same level and that is why the commission's trade strategy is so important to reverse this trend but it is necessary to take advantage of all the important issues that you write about. to actually take on the fight against trade barriers. complicated rules of origin, for example. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. because it will cost lives in poor countries.

in writing. - i voted against the report. the eu's trade policy is basically driven by the profit interests of europe's big companies. the trade agenda under commissioners mandelson, de gucht and now malmström are examples of this. perhaps with the difference that the commission has in recent years been a little more soft on its wording, it has been taken in by the growing popular criticism of trade agreements such as ttip. but in actual practice, nothing has changed. the report supports the policy pursued by the commission, and supports, among other things, the undemocratic ttip and tisa agreements currently being negotiated, including the specialised courts (ics) where large companies will be able to sue states. the report also contains some positive statements, for example on the ilo's involvement, csr requirements and the role of unctad. but in essence, the report is a continuation of support for the commercial policy guided by the profit interests of the big european companies, which is why i voted against it.
in writing. ‒ the european parliament voted today in favour of a non-legislative report 'on the fight against trafficking in human beings in the eu's external relations'. i supported this, because human trafficking, in its way of turning people into commodities, is one of the worst human rights violations. it is about the weakest and most vulnerable people in sweden, europe and the world. i welcome the constructive proposals for action contained in the resolution. as kd opposes a european prosecutor's office, i would like to emphasise that this is still the case, as there are documents on the extension of the mandate of the 'future european union general prosecutor's office'. the report also condemns forced surrogacy, but calls on the member states to 'consider the consequences of their own restrictive reproductive policies'. this writing surprises me, since so-called altruistic surrogacy is a controversial phenomenon. moreover, like other medical ethical issues, the issue clearly belongs at national level. since the members of the european parliament have previously expressed their views on the abortion legislation of the eu countries, it is certainly logical to express their views on other medical ethics legislation in the member states. but it is, nevertheless, wrong. i would therefore like to stress that the christian democrats take a critical view of surrogacy.

madam president, i have three points. first of all, in europe, we already have the highest tax load in the world. that means that european companies are paying 10% to 15% more tax than in any other region in the world. this is one of the reasons why we have too little investment and we have a decline in investments. that is why it is important for this to be about fighting illegal tax avoidance, not making it more difficult to invest in europe. second, tax is a national competence. even though i know that very many here would like it another way around, it is a national competence and will remain so. we are not a european tax court. sometimes, when i listen to the words here, i think some colleagues forget that we are not those who are to judge, but those who are to legislate on the issues we are responsible for. third, we need to have cooperation between member states. that is why it is important to stick to the organisation for economic development rules and beps recommendations: because otherwise we create new grey zones, disadvantages for the european economy and better opportunities for those who like to illegally avoid tax.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the idea behind the creation of schengen was to abolish the internal borders between our member states and strengthen the external border. today we are moving towards this. in fact, the external borders for sweden are italy and greece, if we are to take the union seriously. we are now taking a step with this important report, for which i congratulate the rapporteur, a step towards a european management of external borders. what we are achieving is a border control which will protect us against arms smuggling, against drugs, against terrorist activities and all kinds of other things that we do not want. this, i want to make clear, is not about stopping people seeking refuge for their lives from war and persecution. it's a misunderstanding. we liberals wanted to go a little further. we have not yet come all the way, but politics, as we know, is a marathon and there will be more to come.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the proposal is better than the commission's original proposal, but just because something has been improved does not mean that it is good. the fact that decisions can be taken quickly, as the rapporteur stressed, does not mean that they will be good decisions either. rather, they often become worse because they are unthinking. i have always thought that the day frontex's main task is to save lives, instead of shutting people out of europe, i will give them my support. we now had an opportunity to make a change, but we didn't take it, and frontex plus is not getting my support today either. when we began our term of office, we were told that we should not legislate so much as apply what we already have. instead, there will be a torrent of hastily drafted proposals. this took six months, you said, and speed means, as i said just now, it's not quality at all. what we are dealing with now is that the fight against terrorism and migration has become our most important task in the eu, and i cannot agree with that. commissioner avramopoulos said that people should come in by legal routes, but then we have to create such roads first.
madam president, it took only a few months to agree on a common european border and coast guard, which is urgently needed to control our external borders better, manage migration flows and combat terrorism. this shows to the citizens of europe that, on key issues which concern them, such as migration and security, the eu is able to deliver fast. effective control of our external borders is also a precondition for, and paves the way for, restoring schengen. the beefed-up frontex should be an operational tool to support member states in saving lives, fighting smugglers and traffickers, registering migrants with fingerprints, sharing information and now also assisting member states in return operations. i welcome the fact that responsibility for police and border guards continue to be a national competence and that sovereignty is respected and shared responsibilities clearly defined. the council can trigger a joint operation with transparent decision—making procedures. let me conclude by saying that i am happy that we managed to include children’s rights in the new rules.

in the light of the above, the commission considers that the proposal for a regulation should be amended to include the possibility of a reduction in the amount of the surplus in the community budget. higher revenues do not automatically mean higher eu spending. budget surpluses mean that the eu has demanded high fees from the member states based on the final budget, and these extra revenues should be returned to the member states.
‒ for us moderates, it is self-evident that the 2015 budget surplus should be transferred to the 2016 budget, provided that such transfer also implies a corresponding downward adjustment of the member states' contributions to the budget. we therefore support this resolution, but we would like to stress that we do not support the demand that future transfers of surpluses should instead mean an increase in the eu budget. we are not solving europe's problems by spending more money at the eu level.
‒ while the proposal for a european border and coast guard agency contains some positive aspects, such as the complaints mechanism, the proposal is mainly about extending the powers of the eu border agency frontex. member states with large inflows of refugees may be forced to intensify their border controls in order not to be excluded from schengen. frontex is also given a mandate to carry out border surveillance activities in the eu's neighbouring countries. the main aim of the proposal is therefore to reduce the influx of refugees into europe and to return irregular refugees to their countries of origin or transit. in addition to undermining the sovereignty of member states and preventing refugees from seeking asylum in europe, the proposal further shifts the eu's responsibility for refugees to countries outside europe where they are at greater risk of human rights violations. despite the efforts of the green group to improve the text, the compromise text still contains parts which we cannot support. we are rather in favour of a border management in europe which respects human rights and promotes, rather than hinders, the protection of refugees. we need to focus on life-saving operations for people in distress at sea and ensure that those who reach our borders are able to exercise their right to seek asylum. border controls must never lead to people being denied access to protection in europe.

‒ cramer on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council amending regulation (ec) no 1406/2002 establishing a european maritime safety agency i voted against the report. the report calls for the european maritime safety agency to cooperate with the european border and coast guard agency (superfrontex) and the european fisheries control agency in the surveillance of member states' borders. this report is linked to the new superfrontex and aims to ensure that more authorities monitor the borders. the eu continues to dictate to member states how border and coast guard authorities should carry out their work, and we do not agree with that. that is why i voted against the report.
‒ cadec on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council amending council regulation (ec) no 768/2005 establishing a community control body for fisheries. i voted against the report. the report calls for the european fisheries control agency and the european maritime safety agency to cooperate with the european border and coast guard agency (superfrontex) in the surveillance of the borders of the member states. the eu continues to dictate to member states how border and coast guard authorities should carry out their work, and we do not agree with that. that is why i voted against the report.
‒ we welcome the efforts to ensure an independent relationship between olaf and its monitoring committee. however, we question the decision to transfer responsibility for the appointment of the monitoring committee and its budget to the commission itself, since olaf acts as the supervisory authority for the commission. it would be more appropriate if the responsibility for the monitoring committee fell to the council of ministers or the court of auditors.
‒ the report contains parliament's views on the revision of the long-term budget for 2014-2020. in the long term, i believe that the overall agricultural budget should be reduced so that agriculture becomes more market-oriented. nevertheless, in this report i have voted against reducing the appropriations for the agricultural budget because it would create too much uncertainty for those who receive the funds if the budget were reduced during the agreed programming period. i have also voted against further funding for the iter research project, which i believe has already been over-budgeted without showing any concrete results.
‒ we voted against the report on the revision of the multiannual budget because we cannot support the european parliament's demands for a higher budget, looser budgetary rules and taxation rights for the eu. increased spending at eu level will not solve the problems facing europe. instead, it is important to focus on developing and preserving the single market, deepening trade relations with other parts of the world and pursuing structural reforms to strengthen europe's competitiveness. to do so, it is crucial that we prioritise eu spending and reallocate funds to areas that help us achieve these goals. higher taxes, more spending at eu level, looser budgetary requirements and new eu taxes do not solve any of europe's challenges, but risk creating new ones. for the future, it is important that the eu invests in research and innovation, digitalisation and growth creation, and infrastructure that connects our continent. subsidies to old factories, to farming and fishing fleets around europe, on the other hand, do not contribute to growth and new jobs. in this context, the revision of the long-term budget lacks the right priorities to contribute aggressively to growth and competitiveness.

‒ i voted in favour of the objection to the authorisation of health claims on energy drinks, as i consider them to be misleading and encourage increased consumption. it is not primarily the caffeine in energy drinks that is unhealthy, but the sugar that is associated with, among other things, obesity and diabetes. but health claims on food require that the whole contents be considered, not just individual substances. in this case, this is particularly problematic, since one of the main target groups for these products is children and young people. i do not think that the sale of energy drinks should be banned altogether, but only that claims aimed at increasing consumption should also take into account negative aspects. i hope that the commission will complete its task of developing nutrition profiles to improve the use of health claims as soon as possible.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me start by thanking this house, and in particular the two rapporteurs, for their supportive work on these two important files. starting with the langen report, i am very glad that this house shares our commitment to promoting a more market—oriented, flexible and decentralised energy system providing the right investment signals for the energy transition. ultimately we need a system that puts prosperity and the decarbonisation of our economy at its very heart, and our review of market design rules will be key to that end. on the one hand, thanks to the three consecutive legislative packages, we have introduced competition to varying degrees in different markets and more cross-border flows of electricity. but, on the other hand, we have not yet managed to adapt our market framework sufficiently to the challenges that decarbonisation, and in particular the massive roll-out of variable renewable generation, have brought along. adapting our energy system to the new challenges will require action on at least four fronts. first, we have to ensure that the new market design will work for consumers by making them more active market participants. innovative and smart technologies are available but they are not properly used in many cases because the right regulatory framework is not there. wholesale and retail markets must be more closely linked, giving consumers access to a wider choice of products, and consumers must be able to market their flexibility by removing obstacles for aggregators to enter the market. second, we need electricity markets to give the right investment incentives for a successful energy transition by strengthening price signals. this means the price must be able to rise when demand is high or generation is scarce. constraints on pricing must be removed, as a matter of the first order, so as to reward flexibility. it is in this context that our reforms will be aimed at promoting shorter markets. the integration of the european union—wide intraday and cross—border balancing markets must be significantly accelerated. third, a more integrated energy market will also require us to find new ways of deepening our cooperation across borders on all levels. but full market integration will also require the seamless cooperation of transmission system operators (tsos) at operational level, and we will need to look at ways of strengthening regional tso cooperation based on existing approaches. likewise, more cooperation between regulators will be needed to match closer tso cooperation and this includes reviewing the role of the agency for the cooperation of energy regulators (acer). lastly, while the aim of this exercise is to strengthen the internal market, public policy interventions are likely to remain a part of the reality. we will therefore need a stronger framework to ensure that such measures are compatible with the internal market. the most urgent issue is dealing with the generation adequacy policies that we see emerging in a number of member states. in many cases these follow a purely national logic and focus exclusively on introducing measures to remunerate national generation capacities, without taking account of the benefits of market integration. this is not a viable approach and, if left untouched, it seriously risks distorting the internal energy market. that is why we will propose a european or regional framework for capacity remuneration mechanisms. the main purpose of such a framework will be offer a common approach to assess the adequacy of energy systems. from the commission’s point of view, it is crucial that such an assessment takes into account the contribution of capacity across the border to security of supply. likewise, when a capacity mechanism is introduced, such cross-border capacity, as well as demand response, must be able to participate. as a principle, this is already the case but with our proposals it must become a reality. last but not least, let me pick up on another key point mentioned in the report. it is all well and good to update our market design, but we must absolutely commit ourselves to ensuring the effective implementation of the system legislation. that will continue to be a priority for the commission. we will of course look at the report in detail as we prepare our proposals and i look forward to coming back to this house to present them later this year. turning now to the gierek report on heating and cooling, this report highlights the fact that this sector could be an absolute game changer when it comes to energy security, economic growth and reaching our energy and climate targets. the report places emphasis on the ‘energy efficiency first’ principle and the push for renewables to replace fossil fuels. it focuses on building renovation and the deployment of modern and smart technologies and systems, and highlights the need to reinforce the capacities of citizens, industries and public authorities through better information, awareness, training and education, research and innovation and targeted capacity—building initiatives. these all go in the direction of the commission. let me remind you that heating and cooling in our buildings and industry account for half of european union energy consumption and are largely powered by fossil fuels. seventy-five percent of our buildings are energy inefficient and are mostly fitted with old boilers with low efficiency rates. that is why our study focused on removing barriers to decarbonisation in buildings and industry. we need to link up heating and cooling to electricity networks and to renewable energy more effectively. that will reduce costs for consumers and provide storage for valuable energy. our market design proposals will provide the flexibility and demand response to make that happen, and our upcoming review of the energy performance of buildings directive will prepare the ground for making buildings smart and ready for the new solutions. under the revised renewable energies directive, we will be aiming at further integrating renewable energies of all types in this sector, but we also need to speed up renovation rates. our implementation work, combined with the upcoming review of the energy performance of buildings directive and the energy efficiency directive, will strengthen the drive to replace inefficient boilers faster and incentivise renovations in multi—apartment buildings. to do that we also need to make the right financing available. the good news is that we have started to do that through the european fund for strategic investments and we will back that up with an initiative to help facilitate access to new innovative funding mechanisms. both of these reports highlight how important it is that we get on with building the energy union that this house has supported since day one. the reports in our debate here today are a solid basis from which we can get to work on the whole range of legislative files that are coming up in the next weeks and months.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the climate policy debate, we usually talk about emissions from industry, electricity generation or transport, but the fact is that one of the biggest sources of emissions is heating and cooling. almost 50% of the energy we use in the eu is used for this purpose, and 75% of it comes from fossil sources. to remedy this, district heating is an invincible tool - something that sweden also happens to be an excellent example of. despite being a large and sparsely populated country, 90 percent of all multi-family homes are connected to district heating, and there are no fossil fuels at all. as we heard in the debate, however, the conditions vary enormously in different parts of europe. that is why, in my work on this strategy in the committee on the environment, i have focused on the fact that the eu should not be a detail manager or lock itself into a specific solution. because then we'll never be able to succeed. another thing that is almost as important is to see the potential of biomass from the forest to generate district heating and also to dare to see the possibilities of energy recycling, because if we start fighting it, the way many people now want, then we are stuck in fossil fuels.
mr president, i would like to thank you. if we are to succeed in the green transition of europe's energy system, the electricity market needs to be organised in such a way that we use resources more efficiently. our historical national boundaries are rarely the best geographical division for our energy markets, which could be allowed to function better with new geographical divisions. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the energy market is not affected by the crisis. when the sun does not shine in one country, wind power can be used in another, or water power in a third. meeting the climate challenge requires an energy market and its design that produces more renewable energy. that's what we greens are fighting for.
mr president, heating and cooling represent the largest share in the eu’s energy demand. if you want that demand not to ruin the climate goals we set, we will have to solve the existing problems. as an example, two thirds of the eu’s buildings were built when energy efficiency requirements were non-existent, and nearly half of buildings have individual boilers that were installed before 1992. that is why improving the heating and cooling sector all around the eu is going to play a crucial role in reaching the goals of the paris agreement but, in order to achieve that, eu member states should be pushed to do more, to do something concrete, to support concretely innovation, energy prosumers, households, local enterprises that are constantly monitoring their energy consumption, that produce energy they sell back to the grid. if there is concrete support for these rising stars of the european union, then maybe we can achieve the goal that we set; otherwise it is just empty talk which will end in a surprise.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the upcoming elections in bosnia and herzegovina have led some politicians to push for a referendum on the day of unity in one of the country's two entities, republika srpska. this is despite the fact that the bosnian supreme court has declared it to be contrary to the country's secular constitution. i have some limited understanding of political campaigns in the pursuit of voters, but playing with such things in bosnia can end very badly. this referendum not only challenges the country's sovereignty, security and constitution, but it spreads fear of armed conflict. we can't let this flame flare up again and send this country back to the '90s. no elections, no voters are worth jeopardizing the hard-won peace in the balkans. the commission must therefore act. if we do not show now that peace and stability are not negotiable, we can never again ask others to do so. the commission must demand that this illegal vote be stopped immediately.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the g20 summit had a very ambitious title: towards an innovative, vital, connected and inclusive world economy. i was glad that global leaders understood that the vast challenges of today require such ambition. unfortunately, the good news stayed with the headline. the final declaration does not contain a single binding commitment, but rather is proof that the 20 largest economies do not know how to agree on how to solve today's challenges. how else is the following sentence to be interpreted: we support efforts to promote voluntary knowledge diffusion and technology transfer on mutually agreed terms and conditions. i mean it! not a word at all about who, when, how, with whom. we live in an age of enormous populism. we desperately need growth to move us out of the xenophobic patch we're stuck in. we need to deal with the refugee crisis, syria, russia, climate, epidemics, racism and the only thing the 20 largest economies agree on is voluntary knowledge diffusion. i mean it! colleagues, the g20 summit demonstrates the lack of common denominators and shows that the eu and g20 leaders are not the adults of the mission. no wonder voters are voting for populists who promise gold and green forests. we need concrete actions, measurable results, reasonable and thoughtful responses to today's challenges and the determination to meet them. it's the only way to win the trust of the voters.
madam president, the g20 meeting was held at a time that is more uncertain and more risk-laden than we have seen for decades. it could have been an important meeting in this perspective, but very little came out of it. and what came out of it did so because all the crucial problems that the global world order is facing were avoided. nothing was really said about syria, with its global impact not only on refugees but also on stability and security. nothing was said about ukraine, with the breaking of international law and international agreement that is undermining the rules underpinning world stability. nothing was said about the need to fulfil western sanctions against russia, which is breaking international rules. the conflicts on the islands in the south china sea were also avoided, as were, in reality, the issues about obligations for the countries to fulfil to combat climate change. nothing was said, either, about the need to enhance legal certainty for investments in the global order. we are today facing the undermining of respect for international rules. we see this very much in russia and in china, who say they will not follow international court decisions. the next time there is a g20 meeting it will be important for the european union to take the lead in restoring legal certainty and respect for international rules, because otherwise these meetings have no real meaning.
