blue-card answer. – i think it could have been a good idea also to mention what has happened in turkey in two ways: first of all, to strongly react against any sort of attempt to have a coup d'état, because what could have followed that would have been a civil war with a real disaster, even more than the disaster we are facing. secondly, i think the importance of turkey coming back to full respect for civil society and independence could have been stressed. i think both things should have been said, but the g20 avoided things that were crucial and i do not think that, in the time we are living in, we can avoid the complicated issues. we need to deal with them, hopefully together, but at least together with democratic nations.
in writing. we social democrats voted today in favour of the report on a new market model for the energy market. the report highlights several good points and a new market model for the energy market is necessary in view of the developments in the field of renewable and flexible energy. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks of chemical pollution caused by certain dangerous substances. we see no need for harmonised eu action in this area, and it may threaten the independence of local, municipally owned energy companies with their own production. eu rules already exist, which provide common rules for member states in the field of water activities, and the rules of the internal market promote competition. we don't see the need for any further action. if new measures are warranted, then there are other major problems to be addressed in the electricity market. we also voted against a report on what are allegedly better market-oriented so-called investment promotion measures to promote renewable energy. the generalised wording is unclear and leaves open the interpretation of what is to be considered as different investment measures.
in writing. ‒ i do share the rapporteurs approach that a ‘strengthened european internal market in electricity is possible’. indeed, lots have been done in that regards. today, new electricity suppliers can enter member states’ markets, while both industrial and domestic consumers are now free to choose their own suppliers, something that was completely unthinkable some decades ago. however, the way for a complete, full-fledged internal energy market is still long, and further steps are needed. we need to continue pushing eu member states for a full implementation of the third energy package. full and correct implementation of such legislation will bring more consumer protection, enhance the unbundling of energy networks and promote a better functioning of retails markets. however, the rapport is a good step forward and i welcome it.
in writing. ‒ i voted in favour of this report which highlights the principle that macro-regions are frameworks for cooperation activities and developing synergies between different eu instruments. alpine regions can be considered as a symbol of european integration since it exists a long lasting tradition of cooperation, with a number of networks already in place, and the ambition of this strategy should strengthen this solidarity. experience shows that success highly depends on commitment. we therefore also need strong active involvement of all regional and national partners to fully exploit the potential highlighted in the report.
‒ today the european parliament voted on its position in the commission's ongoing work on what is known in the eu context as 'work-life balance'. the report makes very far-reaching demands for supranationality, not least in the area of family policy. of course, it is important that a pregnant woman is offered good insurance during her pregnancy, that discrimination in the workplace is combated and that there are well-functioning parental insurance systems. but this is not a matter that should be left to the eu. i therefore voted against the report.
in writing. we need to improve the balance between work and family life in the eu and give women and men equal opportunities to combine career and family. however, it is up to the member states to design the systems and conditions which make this possible, in particular leave for working parents. i could not support the report because it calls on the commission to propose maternity leave, paternity leave and leave for carers at eu level. in addition, the report proposes that we should decide on certain types of tax policy, minimum wages and social security systems at eu level, all of which should be decided at national level. we should raise awareness, exchange best practice and encourage member states to pursue a modern gender equality policy that does not see women as the sole parent responsible for childcare. we must focus on combating discrimination and stereotypes against women and girls both in and outside the workplace. we still have a long way to go, but it is not by introducing family and labour market policies from brussels that we will achieve equality in europe.
‒ i voted against this resolution because, apart from the fact that these are key issues which are of enormous importance to me at national level, it is a typical example of a policy which should be made as close as possible to the parties concerned.
in writing. today we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report on the creation of favourable labour market conditions for work-life balance. the report is good in its overall view of an eu where the labour market has been modernised and has become increasingly digital. while we see opportunities, we also believe that in the face of a modernised labour market, caution is needed to avoid reproducing destructive gender roles through, for example, atypical forms of employment in which women are now overrepresented. that's why we voted against items that we believe would cement traditional gender roles. we voted against the proposals that part of parental leave should be transferable. since very few men take parental leave, we believe that instead we need to strengthen parental leave to encourage more men to take advantage of this opportunity. we also abstained on the vote on the item which raised the 'care points' for care of relatives. reducing the gender pension gap and ensuring fair pensions requires other types of measures, such as increased employment and the introduction of the equal pay principle, which would reduce the pay gap. in addition to the written comments, we believe that the report contains a number of concrete comments. the report, in its holistic approach, contains well-founded proposals on combating child poverty, safe forms of employment and increasing gender equality, all of which are moving in the right direction.
in writing. we need to improve the balance between work and family life in the eu and give women and men equal opportunities to combine career and family. however, it is up to the member states to design the systems and conditions which make this possible, in particular leave for working parents. i could not support the report because it calls on the commission to propose maternity leave, paternity leave and leave for carers at eu level. in addition, the report proposes that we should decide on certain types of tax policy, minimum wages and social security systems at eu level, all of which should be decided at national level. we should raise awareness, exchange best practice and encourage member states to pursue a modern gender equality policy that does not see women as the sole parent responsible for childcare. we must focus on combating discrimination and stereotypes against women and girls both in and outside the workplace. we still have a long way to go, but it is not by introducing family and labour market policies from brussels that we will achieve equality in europe.
first vice-president of the commission. – mr president, let me briefly reflect on some of the remarks that have been made. first of all, let me be very clear. if any information pertaining to this issue is brought to our attention, we will carefully study it and reflect on it and take a position on it. we do not disregard any information. if it is academic information, give it to us and we will analyse it and give our point of view. secondly, let me reflect for a moment on the nature of the rule of law: why is it so important? it is because we have learned through history, through a very painful history in europe, that in order to have a democratic pluriform society functioning and the rights of everyone respected, we need the virtuous tripod of democracy, rule of law and full respect for human rights. you cannot give in to the temptation to use one against the other: all three are equally important. and, as an essential part of this, the independence of the judiciary is of extreme importance for respect for individual rights and also for the functioning of our form of market economy. this is nothing to do with politics. politics can lead the electorate to make a different choice, and then governments make different programmes go in a different direction, but they need to stay within the remit of the rule of law, which is full respect for the position of the judiciary in our member states. so, then, this brings me to a number of very precise questions that mr legutko clearly does not want to go into. if you accept my premise that the independence of the judiciary is an essential part of respect for the rule of law, how is it that in poland, the executive can choose whether it wants to publish the rulings of the highest court in the land according to whether it likes the rulings or not? (applause) that is not in line with the principle of the independence of the judiciary. mr legutko said, just for you, mr president, that what i am saying is not true, but let me remind him that several rulings of the constitutional tribunal have still not been published by the polish government. that is a fact, mr legutko. secondly, let me also add that if a parliament decides lawfully to elect members of a court, then the fact that such a lawful election has been followed by other elections does not entitle you to use the new composition of the parliament to undo a legal decision by the previous parliament. so these are the issues we are discussing in a constructive way with the polish government. let me end on one thing which is also a bit personal, i have to say. my professional life, from the very start as a student, was focused on bringing an end to the divide in europe and bringing down communist dictatorship in half of europe. the polish people have achieved that. it is one of the biggest victories in european history, and i will not be accused of acting against the polish people. this is a dispute between a tribunal and a government, nothing more and nothing less. but let us remind ourselves of the beauty of the victory of democracy and the rule of law against oppression and communism, and let us not forget that this is the true, true achievement of our common european dream. (applause)
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, i thank all those who intervened today. i will not comment on those interventions but rather i want to reassure you that i will take back with me the strong interest shown in this house for what is, i hope, our common objective of upholding values on which the european union is built and, above all, the rule of law. i take note of everything. i have nothing to add to my earlier statement on behalf of the council. i also thank commission vice—president timmermans for an update by the commission on poland’s stance in its dialogue, and many thanks to the commission for reassuring us of its readiness to continue this dialogue. we, as the council, will follow this and further steps attentively.
in writing. – the eu has to promote human rights and defend the rule of law. we have let hungary under viktor orbán slide down the path to autocracy. the combat for human rights and respect for the rule of law must be applied systematically. an article 7 procedure should be launched against both poland and hungary. human rights can never be cast aside in pursuit of the rule of law, or vice versa. today women are denied the right to their bodies in poland and in other eu countries. recent initiatives taken in poland are aimed at restricting sexual and reproductive rights even more by completely banning abortion. article 1 of the charter of fundamental rights states the inviolability of human dignity and the right to physical integrity. the charter clearly states that we women are to be considered as individuals in our own right, free to make our own choices concerning our life, sexuality and health. i call on the european union to take a clear stand: the developments in poland, when it comes to fundamental rights for women as well as the rule of law, are in breach of the values of this union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by condemning the attempted military coup in turkey this summer. whatever i think about the way the turkish president is running the country, he is elected by the people and that should be respected. i also condemn all forms of violence. both the violence committed by president erdogan's opponents and the psychological and physical violence the president now exercises against all his opponents. the methods we've been testing this summer seem more medieval than 21st century. i cannot accept journalists being arrested, judges and teachers being sacked, travel bans being imposed, etc., etc. the president is crossing borders in his pursuit of dissent. most recently, he has taken over 28 municipalities that are also governed by democratically elected officials. 24 kurdish mayors and four from the gülen movement. he himself shows no respect for the rules of the democratic game but expects to be respected. it is now more important than ever that we show that we are clearly on the side of democracy in turkey, and democracy also includes respect for human rights.
mr president, let us be clear: the failed coup on 15 july 2016 was an attack against democracy, in which 265 people lost their lives in front of tanks. i regret that the eu was slow in denouncing the coup and in supporting the elected government. it is imperative now to stand by the people of turkey and show understanding for the trauma they went through. it is also important that the rule of law and human rights are fully respected, while bringing those responsible to justice, and, of course, important to remain firm on freedom of expression. but it is by engaging with turkey that we will achieve results towards democracy. by just condemning and criticising emotionally we are adding fuel to the fire and polarising people. it is time to intensify dialogue with turkey on all issues of common concern. that is why it is urgent to relaunch the joint commission with turkey, which has been blocked for two years, on an open agenda post coup. the best way to advance reforms in the judiciary and fundamental rights is to open chapters 23 and 24. turkey is not backing away from the migration deal and neither should we. we should not punish children and women because of erdogan. i welcome the commission’s decision to disburse meal cards for the three million refugees who are welcome in turkey but not everywhere in europe. visa liberalisation should be a merit-based process with no double standards against turkey. again, we have to engage with turkey to break the deadlock on the terror law and move forward. turkey is a crucial partner for us. we need to restore trust and to build bridges. we cannot afford to let the people feel let down. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – i said in my intervention very clearly that the rule of law and human rights should be respected and that we have to remain firm on freedom of expression. i did not say that there is rule of law at the moment in everything that is happening in turkey. what i am saying is that, in order to achieve results in a country that is absolutely crucial for the european union, and to make sure that the people of turkey do not feel let down by europe, we need to engage in dialogue. by just criticising, attacking and condemning a proud nation we will not achieve results. will that make it more democratic? no.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, according to the unhcr, more than 85% of the 60 million refugees in the world live in developing countries. that should be kept in mind when we are discussing the 1.5 million refugees who made it last year to this continent. migration is a global issue and therefore i am very happy that the united nations has now decided to call for the summit next week. i am personally happy to lead the libe delegation there. i look forward to working closely with the commission and european players in ensuring its success. europe has a proud history of granting asylum and defending the fundamental rights of migrants. during these last years, however, we have seen how rising tides of xenophobia and populism have also had a detrimental effect on the european debate. we have unfortunately seen how principles of asylum law, such as the respect of non-refoulement, the right to family reunification and the right to apply for asylum have come under pressure in the european union as well. during the summit i hope that we can once again unite around the principles of humanity and tolerance that were the basis for the creation of the geneva convention and other international standards for the protection of refugees. we must also be much better at tackling the root causes that force people to flee in the first place. but, as long as there are human beings who need protection, those of us who are lucky enough to live in places that people flee to rather than from, need to take our responsibilities seriously. again i encourage all of us in the global approach to migration and i am happy that we are, as a delegation, participating in the un summit. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer. – i would respond that we are in the process, it is work in progress that we are now trying to shape up a good policy for migration and refugees, where every member state has to take its part of the responsibility. i am personally the rapporteur for the dublin regulation that is under reform at this moment, and i am happy to convey a message that europe prevails, a humanitarian continent, a humanitarian union, and that the good principles still do prevail even if they are challenged from time to time.
for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. i had hoped that we would have had both a debate and a resolution today or this week, so that we could also give our input to the un meeting from this parliament. but a debate is better than nothing. when the great floods of refugees from the syrian war began to arrive in europe, we stood with our pants down. there was no preparedness and it also turned out that some countries showed no solidarity whatsoever either with the refugees or with other member states. we tried to solve urgent problems with poorly prepared ad hoc measures. that's why this un meeting is so important. we must solve this problem together. after all, some 60 million people around the world are refugees, not just from war, but also from poverty, political oppression, climate change. if we do not seriously address the root causes, they will only increase. those fleeing poverty are not less worthy than those fleeing war, but they have no legal protection. these are the people that the member states now want to send back home quickly. to continued poverty, to continued oppression, to continued drought. they will not disappear just because we choose to accept only those whom we are obliged to accept under the geneva convention. the risk is that we will create large groups of undocumented people in europe and in the world. in fact, most people leave their homes not because they want to, but because they have to. if we are serious, we must start by helping people not to migrate when they do not want to - both those fleeing poverty and those fleeing war.

the committee on transport and tourism has tabled an opinion. president. - the debate is closed. the committee on transport has of course discussed social dumping in the transport sectors: shipping and ports, trains, air transport, but above all road transport. it is also where we say that monitoring and control of the existing rules is important and must be maintained. the member states and the eu share responsibility. we can do more at eu level. that is why we say that we want the rapid introduction of smart tachographs which, with the help of gps technology, can make controls more efficient. we also want to create an electronic and integrated operator register. we want access to all relevant national registers. if a vehicle is checked on the road in a country, the person checking the vehicle shall be able to immediately connect to the relevant driving licence register, obtain information on the operator and the vehicle inspection record to check the technical status. we need to have transparency in those systems. we also want a common road authority to facilitate monitoring and control and coordination by the member states. all that we are proposing is to bring order to an industry which is important for the common market and to put an end to the exploitation of people which is taking place today.

mr president, i would like to thank you. tomorrow we will decide the future of a trade agreement between the eu and the countries of southern africa. it is a very important agreement which removes virtually all eu customs duties against these countries, but allows the african trading partners to remove their customs duties gradually and in the sensitive product areas not at all. this is very important for regional integration in these african countries. the rules of origin are simplified and trade barriers between african countries are reduced, which has been one of the greatest obstacles to development. this is important because free trade has historically proven to be the best tool for lifting people and countries out of poverty. nevertheless, it seems that there is a risk that a majority of the members of this house will vote against the agreement. however, not only is there a risk of losing all these benefits of the agreement, but there is also a risk that poor countries such as swaziland, namibia and botswana will face higher tariffs against the eu and face the full eu customs wall when their current trade regime with the eu expires in december. yet, many are actually considering it. it is an unholy alliance between those who say that the agreement threatens european production, particularly agricultural production, and those who say exactly the same thing, but that it threatens agricultural production in africa. one of them is wrong. i wonder who are the worst of these groups of protectionists. i have never met anyone who calls themselves a protectionist in this house, but i have met many who say they want to protect one or the other. i have concluded, after careful consideration, that it is probably those who are here tonight and tomorrow when they vote who will say that they are voting against the agreement in order to protect african countries. the members of this house claim to know better than these african countries themselves. this agreement between democracies has been approved by their parliaments and ratified by these countries. yet, it is said that one can sit here and judge and think that it is bad for them, even though they want it. it is not just protectionism, it is neo-imperialism. this time, neo-imperialism comes from the left and is most dangerous. i therefore hope that we will vote unanimously in favour of this agreement tomorrow. africa and europe need it.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the state of the union is under criticism, despite what you are saying, mr juncker. we are seeing a union that is cracking at an ever-accelerating rate and losing more and more trust in the electorate. the biggest lack of trust was in england, which we now know after the election. i would like to take this opportunity to congratulate the british people as they break free from the increasingly oppressive yoke of the european union. i look forward to sweden's continued good relations with the united kingdom. the commission is now advocating defence cooperation, i.e. a joint military force. as you know, sweden has always been a neutral country. it has also enabled us to play the role of credible negotiator in several crises. this is now about to end when we are forced into military cooperation. the lack of understanding of the problems will further erode the confidence of the electorate. at the moment, the eu can be compared to a sick patient who can only be offered pain relief for the moment.
in writing. we swedish social democrats welcome the fact that the stated objective of the economic partnership agreement between the eu and the sadc contracting states (epa sadc) is to contribute to sustainable development and long-term poverty reduction. the fact that the sadc epa is an asymmetrical agreement which takes account of the different circumstances of the contracting parties and enables them to protect sensitive sectors is of the utmost importance to us, together with the reference to the fundamental principles of human rights of the cotonou agreement. we can see that the sadc epa has the potential to make a positive contribution to the social and economic development of the countries in the long term, which is why we chose to support the agreement. we would like to stress, however, that the automatic abolition of customs duties is not a guarantee of positive developments. in order to ensure that the intended benefits of the agreement are shared by the population and contribute to promoting regional integration, the proper follow-up of both the implementation of the agreement and its longer-term consequences in the region is required. it is therefore essential to have in place instruments and procedures to ensure that proper follow-up is carried out in the contracting countries. the european commission must now deliver on its promise that compliance with the sustainability chapter and respect for human rights will be carefully evaluated and that civil society will play a very active role in this follow-up process.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the eu, the world's richest and most prosperous continent, chose this spring to make itself available to the blackmail of an increasingly authoritarian turkish government through the so-called turkey statement - not to be confused with the agreement - rather than trying to solve migration policy challenges within the eu on its own. recent events have made it abundantly clear that the whole idea of relinquishing our responsibility for the reception of refugees to a country like turkey was a failed castle in the air, doomed to failure. after hearing ministers from several countries say that similar agreements should be concluded with countries such as nigeria and libya, i hope that we will now put this behind us once and for all and take responsibility instead. of course we will cooperate with countries outside europe, also on migration, but there should be no doubt that we must put in place our own systems in our own countries to assist in a humane and dignified way those who are entitled to international protection. i think we should work together. we do not need more turkish statements, but a functioning european asylum policy, based on solidarity and shared responsibility.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the humanitarian crisis that has arisen in the wake of the war in syria has now been going on for several years. as a way of resolving the unsustainable situation of many refugees in greece and italy, the commission proposed that asylum seekers be relocated to other member states. the governments themselves have agreed to this and have recognised that we must help the thousands of people who are stranded in greece and italy. but in practice, solidarity stops there and very little happens. as of july this year, only 793 people have been relocated from italy and 2 033 from greece. this inactivity is shameful and totally unworthy of one of the richest regions in the world, which the eu actually is. in addition, the commission and the council are now planning to drain the system. it has been proposed that refugees who have arrived directly in other member states should be able to be 'deduced' from the relocation quotas of these countries. if this is to be a reality, i think it will be the final nail in the coffin of the whole relocation system. i and my group therefore support the rapporteur's proposal. member states must wake up and deliver on their promises to give people who have fled for their lives a chance and a better start in the other eu member states. we must see solidarity, and we must think it could have been us.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have already said a lot of sensible things, but at the same time there is a certain confusion of concepts which i think needs to be clarified. minimum income is not the same as minimum wage. minimum incomes are decided by political assemblies. minimum wages are negotiated between the parties to the labour market. during the crisis years, we have not done much at european level to strengthen trade union rights. rather, we have legislation, and perhaps above all judgments, that erode the ability of trade unions to act. we say a lot, but we do very little. the ilo states the following: collective bargaining is a key means through which employers and their organizations and trade unions can establish fair wages. in the context of the economic crisis, we have seen how countries in europe have restricted trade union rights. at the same time, eurofound says that the countries that are best able to cope with the crisis for each individual citizen are those that are also able to assert trade union rights. it's the way to wealth and wages.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is important that we evaluate how the globalisation fund can benefit more businesses both small and large. one of the issues on which we have unfortunately not been able to agree is the requirement of the number of employees who must be affected in order for the fund to be used. i have a concrete example, namely a food industry in southern sweden, findus in bjuv in skåne. under the current rules, it's uncertain whether we'll be able to mobilize the fund to help the employees of findus. i mean, we have to do something about it. even in cases like this, where so many people are affected, where a whole industry is under pressure, the fund should be able to play a greater role than it does today. we also need to look at how the egf can be used to alleviate the conditions of companies affected by trade conflicts. the commission has previously stated that the fund cannot be mobilised for this purpose. parliament's interpretation is that it can. what does the commission say today? we must be able to agree on the use of the fund. of course, we have also listened to the commissioner for trade, cecilia malmström, who has opened up to such an interpretation. there is talk of cuts in the fund, while malmström thus argues that any negative consequences resulting from trade agreements should be able to be addressed by the fund. i look forward to an answer to that question.

in writing. ‒ i voted against this report. the european globalisation adjustment fund is a clear indication of protectionism. over the last century, it is hard to find systematic evidence for the benefits of protectionism. i firmly believe that fair competence in a context of free markets is the most suitable way to produce wealth for all. i have exceptionally accepted the european globalisation adjustment fund as an emergency measure at time of deep economic crisis. but this time it is meant to be more short-term in its approach, causing a negative impact.

in writing. ‒ we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which suggests that the fund does indeed have a negative impact on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its legitimacy and should not be used any more.
in writing. – in march this year, finland submitted an application for a financial contribution from the european globalisation fund, as 2035 workers, both from microsoft and from downstream producers, were made redundant and lost their jobs. indeed, one of the biggest challenges the eu and its member states face is managing the downsides of globalisation. every one of us is affected, and we really need to alleviate the situation of people facing the negative effects of globalisation. however, i firmly believe that protectionist measures such as the egf would exacerbate the difficulties we are facing rather than resolving them. what we need in europe today are measures that will create jobs, businesses and start-ups – an active strategy to promote competitiveness, innovation, research, development and new technologies throughout the eu. i usually vote against all applications to the european globalisation fund whatsoever. nevertheless, in current times of a severe economic crisis and rising populism, i chose not to vote against and abstained from voting.
‒ the eu globalisation fund is intended to provide grants to people who have become unemployed following the closure of european companies. the christian democrats are opposed to this fund for two reasons. first, labour market policies and financial support for the unemployed should be decided on, and financed, at national level not at eu level. secondly, the fund's basic idea is that globalisation is something negative - which is not true. i therefore consistently vote against the disbursement of the fund. the alliance also repeatedly pointed out during its term of office against the existence of the fund. for ericsson employees who lose their jobs, this is a personal tragedy, but it is sweden that should take responsibility for supporting them in the transition phase to a new job not the eu.

in writing. increased application of intellectual property rights to seed in trade agreements with developing countries may reduce farmers' choices while increasing costs, which in turn may jeopardise food security in these countries. however, a gradual development of intellectual property rights based on experience and voluntary cooperation may be appropriate in certain cases, and that is why we decided to abstain on the amendment on the privatisation of seed. the cultivation of gm crops is in many cases based on the use of herbicide pesticides, which have devastating effects on biodiversity, deplete arable land and, in the long run, threaten sustainable agriculture. we therefore want to see a phasing out of the use of such gm crops, so that food production is gradually replaced by more sustainable alternatives. however, this phase-out must be pursued in a long-term and sustainable manner, which is why we do not support drafts which we fear may complicate the existing food supply in countries.
‒ today the european parliament voted on a resolution on a european public prosecutor's office, with the task of fighting fraud. such a proposal was presented by the european commission during the last term of office and has been a long-standing issue, but has so far not received support in the council of ministers. in sweden, the parliament's justice committee unanimously rejected the proposal in a so-called subsidiarity test. such an exercise of authority is simply considered to be a matter for the national level. i share that view and therefore voted against the resolution.
‒ today the european parliament voted on a resolution on industrial policy, in view of the recent notices in steelworks in france and belgium. i share the spirit of the resolution on solidarity and support for all workers concerned and realise the negative impact on the local communities concerned. i'm afraid that's a completely wrong conclusion. the resolution presents tariffs and supranational industrial subsidies as the way to increase european competitiveness. that's a flawed analysis. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. i therefore chose to vote against the resolution.
in writing; the resolution has positive comments such as that 'the support to eu banks has had no impact on employment or improved the economic outlook'. but at the same time, reference is also made to the europe 2020 strategy, which is linked to the european deadlines and the austerity policy that has long been established within the eu. i would have preferred a resolution that clearly rejects austerity and calls for the eu to adopt an expansionary green economic policy that, through climate-smart investment in industry and the public sector, will get the wheels rolling again. the resolution also includes phrases such as the need to reduce the 'administrative burden' on businesses and stresses the importance of 'abolishing unnecessary legislation'. the absence of such a definition leaves open the possibility of interpretations which could lead to serious deterioration of the working environment and the rights of workers and consumers. the resolution also calls for greater convergence between member states in tax, social and budgetary matters. i am opposed to proposals which have such a federal focus. for these reasons, i choose to abstain.
‒ the resolution on caterpillar and alstom highlights a number of key aspects. these are industries whose operations are challenged by fierce competition in the global market. the scenario is not significant for the industries indicated but applies to all market players. i therefore believe that the resolution takes the wrong approach, praising supranational labour market measures without any real success factors. that is why i am voting no.
in writing. – i support workers who lost their jobs, i share the pain of both workers and their families, and i am aware of the current damage of industrial restructuring. however, i did abstain from voting since i firmly believe that politicians in the eu and its member states should not decide in specific cases, such as caterpillar and alstom. i believe that we need a more ambitious industrial strategy aimed at creating jobs and economic growth and a strategy that is able to provide needed help when necessary. i believe that the authorities should have the power to decide how to implement that strategy in specific cases.

the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. president. - the debate is closed. as late as yesterday we were all sitting in this house celebrating the ratification of the international paris agreement. i am glad that we have succeeded in doing so and that it is in place for the forthcoming climate conference in marrakech. but we shouldn't pat ourselves on the back too much, but we should also understand and realize that this is the beginning. it is now up to the eu and the rest of the world to roll up their sleeves. this is the time to show that we are capable and willing to actually work towards the solutions that are required if we are to reach the goal of limiting global warming to a maximum of 2 degrees and ideally to 1.5 degrees. it takes more than pretty words to do this; it now requires concrete policy. if we are to show the seriousness of the fact that climate change is not an obstacle but a lever for a sustainable society and a more resource-efficient green economy, it is clear that the eu must lead that development. the eu, like all regions of the world, will need to do much more if we are to achieve the goals of the paris agreement. one of the most important tools is the ets, which we have at our disposal. i myself and colleagues in this parliament are currently negotiating the ets. we need to reduce emissions more and faster than we are doing now, but we also need to create the right incentives for industry to be bold in making climate-smart investments. i would also like to stress that the climate change must be a matter for society as a whole. it's not okay for some industries to take a shortcut on others, without contributing to climate change. everyone who can, should do their bit. similarly, we must ensure that the transition is solidarity-based and avoids negative social effects. groups and regions with particular challenges must not be left behind but must be given special support in their transition.
the committee on petitions has been asked to submit an opinion. president. - the debate is closed. as chairman of the committee on petitions, i am constantly reminded that the legislation we are working on here is not always properly implemented in the member states. i am reminded of the importance of its proper implementation, no matter what policy area we are talking about, because it is really about our credibility as institutions; it is about the right of citizens, no matter where they live, in which member state, to actually take part in the legislation we are implementing. unfortunately, the member states leave a lot to be desired in this area, and this applies to all policy areas. we take requests on migration policy, consumer issues, environmental issues and all sorts of other things. we see, from the stream of requests we receive, that there is actually a gap between the intention in the legislation and the implementation out there. as chair of this committee, i will continue to highlight these shortcomings and to stress the need for proper implementation and the right of citizens throughout the eu to have access to the legislation we are working on in an equal way, everywhere.

in writing. ‒ the resolution addresses the humanitarian catastrophe in syria, with particular attention to the situation in aleppo. i welcome the call on all parties, in particular russia and the assad regime, to cease attacks on the civilian population and civilian infrastructure. i therefore voted in favour of the resolution. at the same time, i note that the resolution could have gone further on the subject of russian bombings against civilians. the european union is also calling for the immediate release of all political prisoners and for the immediate release of all political prisoners. this should be emphasized by the european parliament.
in writing. - i voted for the resolution on syria. my basic view is that there is only a political and diplomatic solution to the conflict in syria. there is no military solution to the bloody civil war. the cessation of violence requires a political solution that includes a democratic transition, the handover of power by assad and the guarantee of the rights of syria's various ethnic and religious groups. arms supplies to both the syrian regime and the opposition are an obstacle to a peaceful solution to the conflict. we need a full arms embargo on syria. the un needs to urgently address the crisis and act to protect civilians and curb the flow of arms and resources to isil and other terrorist groups. the forces supporting isis must be put under severe pressure. turkey has a special responsibility for not consistently opposing is, but instead focusing on its own military violence against the kurdish population and its struggle for its fundamental rights. the war in syria has created a terrible refugee crisis. if the eu had taken its responsibility and shown respect for international conventions and human rights, it would have guaranteed the issuing of humanitarian visas to asylum seekers at eu embassies and consulates, and the introduction of mandatory resettlement programmes at eu level instead of the weak voluntary resettlement programme proposed by the council.
in writing. ‒ i am firmly convinced that a resolution is needed to address the serious situation in syria, and in a situation that is rapidly changing for the worse, a new resolution is also needed to address this. i chose to include an amendment from the enf; in this case it was important to also show support for the kurds, who have been affected by the syrian war and by the political leadership in turkey.
‒ cop22 will be an important next step after the successes of cop21. the resolution as a whole is both acceptable and well balanced. i am opposed to the financial transaction tax because it has a counterproductive effect on the movement of capital in the internal market. that is why i voted in favour of deleting this paragraph, in agreement with alde.
in writing. ‒ tackling climate change is one of the biggest challenges we face and it is vital that all countries of the world shoulder their share of the burden. climate finance and technology transfer are important to reduce emissions in less developed countries. but introducing an eu tax on financial transactions is the wrong way to go. giving the eu taxation rights would be a major transfer of power from the member states to the eu and would erode the swedish tax base. we will continue to decide on tax policy in sweden on our own. that's why the moderates have voted against the introduction of such a law.
in writing. for us, there is no doubt that climate change is the fate of our time. we therefore wholeheartedly support the paris agreement and its objective of limiting the increase in global average temperature to below 2 °c, with further efforts to limit the increase to 1.5 °c. the objective is absolutely necessary and an investment in the future of humanity and nature. the eu's commitments under the paris agreement may mean that financial resources for climate action may increase in the short term, and we therefore welcome the commission's undertaking of a comprehensive assessment of the possible impact of the agreement on the eu budget. however, we believe that there is a lack of clarity in the text on an automatic financing mechanism at eu level for this purpose and have therefore refrained from supporting such a proposal.
‒ harmonising legislation on materials intended to come into contact with food is of great importance for the internal market and for european consumers to feel confident that the food they eat is safe. that is why we voted in favour of the report. however, we do not share the unfounded criticism of the european food safety authority (efsa) contained in the text. nor can we support calls to ban or phase out substances without scientific support.

in writing; the report deals with the commission's annual report on monitoring the application of union law. it shows, inter alia, that the environment, transport, the internal market and services remained the policy areas with the highest number of infringement procedures in 2014. the report is critical of the fact that the work of upholding eu law is still not sufficiently transparent. the report also calls for the establishment of a european union-wide network of national authorities responsible for the protection of the environment, and for the establishment of a european union-wide network of national authorities responsible for the protection of the environment. these are good points, and i agree with them. other points raised include the importance of reducing eu scepticism among citizens, without any reflection on why it exists. the main issue of the report is, as the title indicates, the basic functions of the eu, which are profoundly undemocratic but not sufficiently criticized. that is why i have decided to abstain.
mr president, this week we ratified the paris agreement. the challenge of keeping global warming below two degrees will require a substantial reduction in greenhouse gas emissions. this process of decarbonisation provides opportunities for the european economy and industries. however, this transition can also create risks. the european systemic risk board warned about the adverse scenario where the transition to a low carb economy comes late and abruptly. in this case, the cost of the transition will be higher, with severe effects on economic and financial institutions. in other words, our financial institutions’ pension schemes and insurance companies have to adjust to a new reality very soon in order to avoid potential risk for our economic system and economy. this is why it is crucially important that the commission further assesses the systemic risk associated with the low carbon transition and proposes, where necessary, transparency requirements and policies to mitigate systematic risk as much as possible. in that way, the commission will enrich financial markets with more information so the market itself can address the risks. it is necessary to do it quickly and i insist that the commission does not hesitate on that matter.

mr president, i would like to thank you. if i were to come here by train from my second home city, sarajevo, bosnia, instead of travelling from sweden, this journey would take 48 hours. i am very grateful to be a swedish parliamentarian. it's extremely much easier to be. this is, of course, unreasonable. the lack of rail and road connections has created major enormous communication problems in eastern europe and has impeded economic development, damaged jobs, reduced the mobility of goods, services and people and damaged tourism, created problems with energy supply and exacerbated climate issues. so we have to do something about it. i am very pleased that the commission has already proposed a package, which is only to be welcomed. but i hope that more will come, and i hope that the efforts will be swift and that the money will eventually increase, so that the enlargement in eastern europe will be more extensive and the follow-up will be frequent enough so that travel to the region will no longer feel like an absurdity.

madam president, i would like to thank you. in recent years we have seen how country after country in our union has neglected the rights of its citizens. freedom of the press, freedom of speech, the right to love whomever you want, and many of the most fundamental principles of the rule of law have been put at risk by the powerful and populist leaders of the various countries. so the building that once built europe strong in the ruins of the second world war has begun to fade and break down. people are pitted against people and populists and nationalists who want to divide us, divide us and cultivate fear and suspicion instead of building trust. we liberals have for many years and in all the different countries been pushing the question of creating some kind of rights mechanism to ensure that our fundamental rights are guaranteed. that is why i am very happy and pleased today that my colleague, sophia in 't veld, can present this excellent report. it is now important, however, that the commission follow up and make concrete legislative proposals so that we can move forward. because fundamental rights should not be pretty words on a piece of paper that nobody cares about in the member states. governments must now take responsibility. because rights mean something in practice, and rights are the protection of the individual against the collective. we have achieved a great deal in europe and now we are taking the next step.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i would like to begin by thanking sophia von veld for a very ambitious report on a very, very important issue. i think that's one of the most important questions we have to answer. we call it fundamental rights in the eu system, and that is exactly what it is. human and civil rights are the foundation. the defence of these rights must therefore also take precedence over all other considerations. unfortunately, we often see the opposite. i am therefore pleased that this report today is shining a spotlight on the foundations of the european union: fundamental rights, where the rule of law is also the basis of democracy. there is a lot of talk about poland and hungary today. it is quite true that the development of these countries has gone too far in the wrong direction. i believe that an article 7 procedure must be opened against both of them if the eu is to continue to be taken seriously as the defender of the union's own fundamental rights. it is important to recall that violations of fundamental rights may occur in all member states. france is deporting roma who are eu citizens. sweden restricts free movement by means of border controls. the czech republic and slovakia segregate children in schools. in 2000, the eu sanctions against austria ended in failure. almost 17 years later, we're practically in the same situation. long before i took my seat in the european parliament, i was on the barrosso commission on the respect of human rights in the candidate countries. i wondered how the commission was going to ensure that the new member states respected human rights. barroso replied that it was much better if they were involved. it is our duty to protect human rights.
in writing. - i voted for the resolution. i consider the nuclear agreement with iran to be a political and diplomatic success. the agreement reduces tensions in the region and increases the possibilities for promoting peace and security. at the same time, i would like to stress the serious violations of human rights in iran. these include the death penalty, torture, serious discrimination against women in many areas, serious restrictions on freedom of expression and assembly, not least against trade unions. the eu and its member states must, in the newly launched dialogue with iran, make a clear protest against these serious human rights violations and demand democratization. the eu's trade policy, if it is linked to clear demands, could contribute to such a development in iran.
in writing. we swedish social democrats welcome last year's nuclear agreement with iran as an important step towards making the region and the world a safer place. we believe it is important that the eu now responsibly sets out the conditions and the requirements that should be met in order to make a constructive future relationship with the country possible. that is why we voted in favour of the report on a post-deal strategy for iran. we want to stress that respect for human rights must be the first priority and must always be the guiding principle in all eu relations with third countries, as the report on iran also stressed. however, since we considered that several of the amendments, despite their focus on human rights, did not add anything new to the report but rather repeated what was already in the text, we chose not to vote for them.
‒ although i voted in favour of this report, i do not support the proposal on the european public prosecutor's office. nor do i support the demand for mandatory rules that state that convicted persons should not be allowed to take political office or work in public administration. such measures are to be considered as an additional sanction, which is normally only imposed temporarily by a court. otherwise, the convicted persons are punished twice, which is contrary to the principle of ne bis in idem.
in writing. ‒ i can broadly support this report which stresses the importance of combating organised crime, corruption and money laundering. however, in paragraph 42b of the draft report, it was written that people found guilty of the crimes mentioned in this resolution would not be allowed to stand as candidates in elections or to work for a public institution. i think that the system we have in sweden regarding the possibility of standing as a candidate in elections and seeking public office is good and reflects my position of principle. that is why i voted against paragraph 42b.
in writing. we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report on the fight against corruption and the follow-up to the resolution of the special committee on organised crime, corruption and money laundering. point 42 (b) proposes a ban on persons convicted of certain crimes or who have been involved in organised crime from working in public administration in the member states and standing in general elections. we understand that the idea behind the proposal is to fight corruption and to prevent the influence of organised crime in elected assemblies, which is a problem in some member states. we believe, however, that the rules governing who may stand in general elections should be determined in the member states. we also believe that it is important in principle that people convicted of crimes should be given a second chance. we therefore abstained on this point, but voted in favour of the report in the final vote.
‒ this report on human rights and migration in third countries contains several elements that i support, including the focus on the vulnerability of women and children and the importance of international responsibility towards victims of war and conflict. i also chose to go against my own party group and vote for bills on legal ways of migration. however, there are a number of very problematic points in the text. firstly, there is no clear distinction between the terms 'migrant' and 'refugee', as underlined by the rejection of epp proposals supporting the return of persons deemed to be in need of protection earlier in the process. this is unfortunate, as clarity on the definition of who is a refugee and a generous refugee policy that does not include economic migration is a prerequisite for a sustainable migration policy and a generous refugee policy. i therefore voted against the report in the final vote.
‒ this own-initiative report takes important steps towards a sustainable, secure and competitive energy system in the union. it also stresses the importance of ending dependence on external gas supplies, which can indirectly contribute to geopolitical tensions. it is essential that the eu does not impede the emergence of other types of energy, including biogas, in order to take more superficial steps towards a green transition. the availability of lng and improved infrastructure can also contribute to economic benefits, as the current market supply allows for competitive energy prices. prices that can stimulate member states to take the necessary steps in the right direction.
in writing. in the vote on the report on an eu strategy for liquefied natural gas and gas storage, we voted in favour of the texts concerning nord stream 2. we generally share the concern expressed in the report about the implications for security of supply, security policy and eu energy policy that the nord stream 2 project may have. it is therefore important that this be taken into account in the report. we also chose to abstain on the texts in the report which refer to the possibility of increasing energy trade relations with the united states, including through ttip. the ttip negotiations are ongoing, we don't know what a possible final agreement will look like, and a large part of us gas is shale gas extracted by hydraulic fracturing, which can have a major negative impact on nature. the increased supply of liquefied gas on the global market offers opportunities to diversify the eu's energy supply, but at the same time our delegation wishes to underline the importance of reducing the eu's long-term dependence on gas to achieve its climate and energy objectives.
‒ the report makes recommendations on how to ensure that eu fisheries rules are monitored more uniformly by all fishermen in the union. the recommendations also apply to recreational fishermen and this is important, as recreational fishing in some parts of the union has a major impact on local fish stocks. the report calls on the commission to take this into account in future legislation. this does not mean that the administrative burden for swedish recreational fishermen will increase in any way.
in writing. - i voted for the report. the main idea of the report is that the european commission should establish a binding mechanism to report annually on the state of respect for democratic principles, the rule of law and human rights in the eu countries. the aim is to monitor developments in the eu countries so that democracy is not undermined. the new eu mechanism would also provide the opportunity for recurrent debate within the eu institutions and national parliaments on democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights. i would like to emphasize that the monitoring should be limited to these core and structural issues of democracy. the intention is that no eu member state should be able to restrict democracy and/or undermine the rule of law. however, each member state must of course have full freedom to develop the economic and social policy which its government, after democratic and free elections, has been given the mandate to pursue.
in writing. ‒ i supported the report to make monitoring of fundamental rights and the rule of law across the eu more efficient, by building on existing tools and strengthening them, without a formal revision of the treaties. it is important to be able to react quickly when there is a serious risk of breach of fundamental rights or the rule of law in member states. the interinstitutional agreement proposed allows a debate to be opened at a much earlier stage in order to find a solution, before having to arrive at the nuclear bomb of article 7 of the treaties. i also support that the commission remains solely responsible for drafting the annual report on fundamental rights and the rule of law and for triggering any further step in the procedure against member states. the european parliament will also be able to play a role in the monitoring mechanism within its mandate and without becoming a tribunal, differently from what we have seen in previous proposals.
in writing. we swedish social democrats supported the report on recommendations to the commission on the establishment of an eu mechanism for democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights. it is extremely important that we in the eu have better tools to be able to act when a member state systematically violates the values of the eu treaties and infringes fundamental rights. at the same time, we see problems with parts of the report which propose possible treaty changes. two of these proposals would fundamentally change the constitutional order of europe. the relationship between national constitutions and the eu treaties would be changed and the eu's competences would be extended. unfortunately, it was not possible to vote against these proposals alone and we therefore abstained in the votes on paragraph 20, paragraphs 6 and 7, and paragraph 20, paragraphs 1 and 3.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to call for greater responsibility for a fairer distribution of the resources offered by the eu. the recent commission communication on the youth guarantee and the youth employment initiative clearly shows that we need to do much more to reach people who are struggling with poverty, social exclusion, disability and discrimination. including romans. according to a fra study, 58% of young roma are excluded from the labour market, vocational training and education. but the eu funds that exist to give these young people a chance at employment and education do not reach this group. halfway through the mff, it is crucial to review all key financial instruments, such as the esf, erdf, erasmus+ and creative europe, to ensure that those in dire need of these programmes do not face obstacles. i also want us to respect the new report from the court of auditors.
madam president, plant health is a key factor in promoting the sustainable and competitive agricultural sector. we are all aware of the importance of making the eu better protected from the possible entrance of new pests that have devastating effects, such as those we have seen recently in italy and spain. however, the result of negotiations is a system where it becomes so easy for member states to impose bans on plants and plant products based on weak preliminary assessment. i firmly believe that it will make imports more difficult, will lead to the substantial increase of costs, trade distortions, threatening the eu trade in plants worth an estimated eur 30 billion. there must be a balance between import restrictions and the protection of trade interests, otherwise we risk putting in force legislation which can be a clear signal of protectionism.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the ceta agreement with canada is now in serious doubt, as wallonia and the brussels region have now rejected the agreement in its current form. one problem for my country is that our strong animal welfare is being affected by the agreement in a negative way. dispute settlement is also a problem, where multinational companies are given too much power. canada, in particular, has had its own problems with so-called letterbox companies abusing this dispute clause. i believe in free trade agreements and i am convinced that they will bring great export opportunities for my country. however, the whole process has meant that it would have been much more transparent from the very beginning to avoid all the problems we see now. finally, i must say to mr weber and others: the people of europe laugh at you and your constant throwing of the dice. you're acting like a bunch of uncouth, spoiled puppies sitting in a sandbox with your bucket and shovel, and you're pissed off that you can't have your little sandbox in peace. democracy is something you only like if it's your version of democracy.
madam president, i would like to thank you. we heard earlier mr weber compare the eu to a bicycle that must be stepped on to move forward. it makes sense. but my recommendation to mr. weber and the rest of you in here is to hit the brakes instead. park your bike and think about which way you're going to ride. i would rather compare the eu project to a punctured bicycle wheel that can no longer be repaired. it's like everything you do is artificial respiration. we're going to triple the funding for frontex. we're going to have a new frontex. we're going to reform dublin, create new mechanisms, forcibly distribute migrants... i don't know what else you can come up with. the only question is whether the people of the member states know what you are doing. i will at least follow the decision of the united kingdom and take my people to a referendum and leave this building.
in writing. - i voted against the budget. the budget is too large and too comprehensive. there should be major cuts. agriculture is just one example. it results in overproduction and negative effects on the environment, climate and livestock. another example is the unmanageable regional and structural funds, where there is widespread fraud, as the commission itself has confirmed. i am opposed to the continuing efforts to build a fortress europe, which forces people fleeing war and oppression to risk their lives to reach a place of safety. this and the initiatives for the further militarization of the eu are the wrong way forward for a union which claims to defend human rights and is a peace project. as an anti-federalist, i also believe that the member states who pay the eu budget should have full say over the union's expenditure. it is the taxpayers of the member states who pay and therefore it is their directly elected representatives in each country who should have the power. these are some of the reasons why i am voting against the budget.
in writing. we swedish social democrats chose to abstain in the vote on the package deals for heading 1-5 (blocks 1-6) and in the vote on the two reports on the eu budget 2017 (part 1 & part 2). several items in parliament's budget are given high priority. there are well-functioning businesses with clear european added value. investments in research under horizon 2020 and infrastructure under the connecting europe facility are among the best of what is being done in eu cooperation. the general approach should be to minimise the impact of the efsi investment fund on these programmes. we welcome the significant investments in a number of programmes which the committee on budgets - and now the whole of parliament - is advocating. however, as a general rule, we believe that the efforts made should be compensated by appropriate savings in low-priority parts of the eu budget. the general lack of checking of individual appropriations in the budgetary process to reallocate funds to priority areas contributes to parliament's being well above the mff ceiling for the budget as a whole. we cannot therefore support parliament's budget proposal.
‒ today the european parliament voted on the draft eu budget for 2017 by the committee on budgets. the christian democrats are clear that the long-term ambition is to gradually reduce eu spending on agricultural, labour market, regional and industrial policies. since the report wanted to go in the opposite direction, i chose to vote against.
‒ the budget report contained many good elements such as the reinstatement of the efsi research fund and more funds to deal with the refugee crisis. however, the overall budget was an increase both compared to the commission and council proposals, and as i believe that the overall eu budget should not be increased, i chose to abstain in the final vote.
mr president, globalisation has created cross-border problems that need to be addressed in a flexible manner. the priorities of yesterday are not the priorities of today. therefore i welcome the commission presenting a mid-term review. the new proposal is, however, in the words of georgi gotev, an unusual budget. the envelope dedicated to security and migration could be increased by 25%, while money spent on inclusive growth could be decreased by 15%. like my liberal colleagues, i am convinced that revision is highly needed. the european union is currently facing a wide range of crises, notably the immigration crisis, internal emergencies, security issues, agriculture crises, the lack of funding in research and development and a persistent high level of unemployment, especially among young people and third-country nationals. let us therefore redesign our priorities.
in writing. ‒ we believe that prosperity is created through free trade and fair competition. we therefore oppose the european globalisation adjustment fund on principle, which we regard as an expression of protectionism. we have accepted in the past that the fund was used as a purely emergency measure in a time of deep economic crisis, as a way of countering people's sense of hopelessness and the exclusion that follows in the wake of the crisis and that risks further reinforcing the wave of nationalism and xenophobia spreading in europe. however, in particular in the light of the swedish national audit office's analysis, which considers that the fund does indeed have a negative effect on returning to work, we believe that the fund has lost its eligibility and should not be used any more.
‒ unfortunately, this second settlement agreement has become more protectionist than the mandate parliament last gave for negotiations. the final result is that the rules on import restrictions based on plant diseases have moved from being a genuine risk-based assessment based on science to being more open to overly arbitrary assessments and to large administrative burdens for importers. in my view, eu rules to prevent the spread of plant diseases should not be a tool for setting up disproportionate barriers to trade and unfortunately this report has gone too far in my view.
‒ the resolution on trans fats is well balanced and could be voted for. setting a maximum level for the use of trans fats in food is of value to both the centre party and alde. the who study which underpinned the resolution focuses on public health and cardiovascular disease, a widespread problem in the eu, often associated with high intake of trans fats (tfa). the 2% limit is an achievable target and follows the danish example, which is proven and has shown a real reduction of tfa in food. the industry welcomes the limit and considers it feasible. as regards trade in the internal market, it is important to standardise the use of tfa to create incentives for good practice, while the resolution advises against replacing tfa with palm oil which is harmful to the environment, public health and the producer.
in writing. we support the resolution's call on the european commission to limit the levels of industrial trans fats in food. high intake of trans fats is a risk factor in particular for coronary heart disease, which causes approximately 660 000 deaths per year in the eu. despite compelling evidence of the health benefits of limiting industrial trans fats in food, there is a lack of adequate eu legislation on the matter. while we generally welcome food labelling as an important tool to provide consumers with information on the contents of food, we are aware that labelling alone is insufficient to reduce the intake of trans fats by the eu population. it is therefore all the more important that the eu as soon as possible introduce a mandatory eu legal limit value for industrially produced trans fats in all food. such an actual limit value would be an effective tool and would have a wider impact, since it also covers unpackaged food and restaurant food. it is also important that eu action does not encourage food manufacturers to increase the amount of palm oil in their products, as this could lead to high environmental and social costs and serious human rights violations.
author. – mr president, commissioner: europe should mean safety for migrant children. there are more and more children coming to europe, and they are younger and younger: 80 000 only this year, out of which 70 000, according to unhcr, are accompanied minors. behind every number there is a tragedy. there is a heart-breaking story. children are now trafficked, abused, exploited and exposed to terrible suffering. it is happening in europe today. we have an emergency of more than tens of thousands of children that are going missing. they are running away from reception centres and becoming victims of organised crime. the police, the judiciary, law enforcement authorities have to prioritise children. we need better rules for cross-border cooperation, better cooperation with europol, better interoperability of the databases, and yes, welcome to the commission proposal to reduce the age of fingerprinting from 14 to 6, because it is a way to bring the children and to keep the children into the protection system. and that is our duty: to make sure they enter the protection system and to build the trust, to make sure that they remain in the protection system, not by detaining them. arbitrary detention of children should not have a place in europe. still we do not have zero tolerance for impunity for abuse of children in europe, and it is about time that we build this policy. children should be identified, registered and given the right to ask for asylum. member states should accelerate the procedures for granting asylum for children. fast-tracking, relocation and resettlement programmes should be giving priority to children. i want to conclude by saying that i met, in sweden, afghan children, in an organisation they have themselves built, helping each other. let us start to change the rhetoric. they can be an asset and a benefit to our society if we give them access to education, to health care, to psychological support and to dignified shelter. they are an opportunity for our societies.
for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. children who flee alone without adult support are the most vulnerable people on european soil. we see their vulnerability and how they are at risk of being exploited and exposed to violence and exploitation by unscrupulous adults. many people disappear, and we know that europol reports that 10,000 have disappeared in the last year. most shocking and saddening of all is the sight of the lonely children who have lived in the so-called calais jungle, despite the fact that they have the right to be reunited with their families in britain. this is a disgrace for the european union, because behind the anonymous faces with empty eyes turned towards the television cameras that come to visit there is a name, a destiny, a story. in my role as rapporteur for the dublin regulation review, we will try to find specific ways of ensuring the safety and security of these children. no child should live in the dustbins of europe, alone in the cold and rain, potential victims of unscrupulous adults. no one on earth has the right to take away their childhood in this way. our mission is to give them their childhood back. to give them life, hope and a future. no more, no less than that.
